Three Ways to Avoid CAPTCHA Prompts When Using a VPN

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

VPNs make it harder to track your online activity across websites, but it also makes them suspicious about your origins. When you borrow an IP address that hundreds of other people are also using for any number of reasons, you’re much more likely to get repeat verification requests from online firewalls.

That said, you don’t have to keep clicking on those traffic lights over and over. You can simply switch to a more reputable VPN server. Moreover, browsers now offer persistent access tokens to eliminate CAPTCHA requests, while VPNs offer dedicated IPs to keep sites from getting suspicious. Here are three easy fixes for repeat CAPTCHA prompts when using a VPN, so you don’t have to trade convenience for online privacy.

CAPTCHAs are simple puzzles that websites use to filter out bots and spam. They’re supposed to be easy for people but hard for robots, such as simple object recognition tasks or audio-visual challenges. However, they can still disrupt your browsing experience if they happen too frequently.

Most modern CAPTCHA providers, like Google's reCAPTCHA v3 or Cloudflare Turnstile, start working long before they give you a puzzle to solve. It usually starts with a risk score that studies your IP address, browser and device signals, or recent online activity.

If the CAPTCHA provider can’t confidently identify your request as human based on those signals, it requests that you complete a puzzle before deciding if the request should be blocked. That means when you get repeat CAPTCHA requests, there’s something affecting your browser’s risk score. For example, popular VPN servers spread the same IP address across any number of users, which can easily make a website mistake you for a bot farm. If you change your IP address frequently by switching servers often, that might look suspicious too.

Luckily, there are ways to deal with frequent CAPTCHA requests using tools and services available with your browser and VPN. Cloudflare has already cut its CAPTCHA requests by 91% using its Managed Challenge platform. Apple has also been shipping token-based verification on devices since iOS 16 and macOS Ventura. In June 2026, Cloudflare even announced a joint effort called the Private Access Control Tokens (PACT) standard with Chrome, Edge, Firefox, and Shopify.

While there’s no direct off switch for CAPTCHAs, you can use different fixes to improve your browser’s trust signals when using the internet. That way, you’re less likely to be hounded by verification requests.

The easiest fix for repeat CAPTCHAs is to stop connecting to whichever server your VPN app picks automatically. Proton recommends choosing servers with lower traffic load. In fact, its apps display load as a percentage to help you avoid the most crowded options. I'd also skip big traffic hubs like New York or London, since that's where most traffic originates from. Servers in smaller cities located nearby are often just as fast but never as populated.

You should also avoid changing IP addresses too frequently, which can look suspicious on its own. Pick a server that seems to behave well, then continue using it for at least one session. Also remember you’re more likely to get premium server options if you’re on a paid VPN plan. Major platforms like ExpressVPN, Surfshark, Proton, and Nord are particularly likely to have a larger server collection to pick from.

Costs nothing more than your current subscription

Hopping between servers often can backfire

Private Access Tokens (PAT) let you verify the authenticity of your request using your device rather than an IP address. Apple calls it Automatic Verification, where an Apple server validates your device and Apple Account to assign you an anonymous token.

To enable Automatic Verification on an iPhone or iPad, go to Settings > [your name] > Sign-In & Security > Automatic Verification. On a Mac, open System Settings > [your name] > Sign-In & Security > Automatic Verification.

Unfortunately, there’s no equivalent feature for Windows, Android, and Linux users. That said, Cloudflare offers the Silk extension for Chrome, Firefox, and Edge that serves a similar function. A quick look at the ratings will tell you it’s not as effective, but Cloudflare’s upcoming cross-browser standard called PACT seems promising once it is released.

Doesn’t share personal details with websites

Chrome and Firefox extensions can be unreliable

If you pay a bit extra for a dedicated IP add-on, VPN providers can assign you a unique IP address that isn’t shared with anyone else. For example, NordVPN offers dedicated IPs in 30 countries for $2-5 a month and Surfshark charges $3.75 a month for it.

There are also dedicated providers like Private Internet Access, which charges $3.99 per month for a zero-trust setup so not even their employees know which IP address is assigned to you. On the other hand, Proton VPN offers dedicated IPs only to business plan users, so you’ll need to be on the Workspace Standard, Premium, or Enterprise plans to get one.

That said, there is a trade-off here too. NordVPN itself admits that a dedicated IP makes it easier to link online activity back to a single user. Also, many websites can still tell that the IP address originated from a VPN data center rather than a consumer’s device.

Get unique IP addresses with pristine reputation

Works across desktop, mobile, and even smart TVs

Also functions as an allowlisted address for work

Add-ons cost more on top of the VPN subscription

Offers less anonymity than shared VPN servers

Kim Kardashian’s Beverly Hills Marital Villa Lists for $8.5 Million—Plus 4 More Luxury Homes for Sale

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This week’s standout luxury listings include the Notting Hill blue-door townhouse, Diana Jenkins’s Hidden Hills mansion, and a Connecticut estate by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé.

Even if you keep up with the Kardashians, you might not recognize this Beverly Hills house, which was one of Kim Kardashian’s first real estate purchases. The reality star bought the gated home 16 years ago for $3.4 million, and it served as the setting when basketball player Kris Humphries proposed to her in 2011. But the residence has gotten a major overhaul in the years since Kardashian sold it following the short-lived marriage, and now the Tuscan-style villa is on the market for nearly $8.5 million.

Other listings on our radar this week include another reality TV–linked estate: A former Real Housewife of Beverly Hills is parting with her 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills house. On the East Coast, a striking Connecticut home by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé is also on the market. And across the pond, the memorable blue-door townhouse from ‘90s rom-com Notting Hill in London is also for sale. Check them all out below.

One of Kim Kardashian’s earliest real estate purchases was this gated property in Beverly Hills, which she acquired in 2010 for $3.4 million. The Tuscan-style villa quickly became a familiar backdrop on her family’s E! reality show, Keeping Up with the Kardashians, and was even the site of Kris Humphries’s (televised) 2011 proposal to her. In the aftermath of their 72-day marriage, Kardashian sold the half-acre estate, and its new owners tapped fast-rising designer Sebastian Zuchowicki to oversee a complete overhaul. Now on the market for just under $8.5 million with Jack Harris and Michael Fahimian of Beverly Hills Estates, the five-bedroom home has a double-height entry foyer, custom kitchen cabinetry, and a primary suite with a fireplace, two walk-in closets, and a soaking tub.

Entrepreneur Diana Jenkins spent much of her time on The Real Housewives of Beverly Hills nesting in her sprawling Hidden Hills mansion with her newborn daughter, Eliyanah. Now, four years since leaving the Bravo reality series, the native Bosnian is returning to her home country, per Realtor.com—and listing the 13,200-square-foot mansion for $23 million with Sotheby’s. Set on 4.4 acres, the seven-bedroom house was built in 2019 and comes with a plethora of perks, among them an infinity-edge pool, barbecue, fitness studio, and home cinema.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. The blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill is now on the market. And the 2,750-square-foot house can be yours for roughly $10 million.

With its organic silhouette, cantilevered roofline, and clever use of space, Leuthold House by Allan Gelbin bears the hallmarks of his mentor, legendary architect Frank Lloyd Wright. Completed in 1966 on five rolling acres in New Canaan, the five-bedroom house spans roughly 5,700 square feet and underwent a restoration by Hobbs Inc. and JMK Architects in 2014. The interiors feature long expanses of glass and mahogany, while the grounds come with a spring-fed pond, a pool, a one-bedroom guest house, and 150 specimen trees. Listed for $6.2 million with John Engel and Melissa Engel of Douglas Elliman, the two-story house is on the market for the first time in 29 years.

One of the art world’s most famous couples once lived and worked in a gated townhouse a block from Washington Square Park. Jackson Pollock and Lee Krasner didn’t own the MacDougal Alley home—a brick-faced carriage house built in the 1870s—but rather stayed there in the winter of 1950 due to the generosity of its owner, a fellow celebrated artist and their friend Alfonso Ossorio. Now, Ossorio’s 1,500-square-foot residence is on the market for the first time since 1988, per the New York Post. The asking price for the two-bedroom, two-bath home is nearly $8 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

With the <i><em>Hunger Games</em></i> Press Tour, Have We Reached the Final Frontier of Method Dressing?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The cast has offered a pointed take on the state of today’s red carpets

Another Hunger Games movie, a prequel to the initial series, premieres later this month, and with it comes another press tour full of fantastically far-fetched fashion. As the promotional events begin to stack up for Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, we are reminded of the familiar lore of this dystopian empire: In a fictionalized future America called Panem, the 12 districts that supply resources to the rich and powerful people of the Capitol are forced to send two children to fight to the death each year. The excess of the Capitol is signaled in the films via outlandish style, which the newest film’s cast members have been attempting to channel in their recent looks. The goal is to be extreme—but in an era of extremes on the red carpet, these over-the-top ensembles actually don’t look so out of place.

Elle Fanning plays a young Effie Trinket, who is essentially a Capitol stylist dressing the young “tributes” in all sorts of fanciful fashions—and doing the same for herself. (In the first film series, the character wore a lot of McQueen.) So far, Fanning’s press looks have deliberately channeled that outlandishness. For a London fan screening, she wore a gown from Robert Wun’s Fall 2026 couture collection that subverted ideas of childlike wonder. Her ensemble began in miniature with a music-box-style ballerina atop a bubblegum pink fascinator. The corresponding human-size gown features a doubled-up bodice, the outer layer appearing to peel off, above a full tulle skirt, all in that same dusty pink.

For her other appearances, Fanning has established a de facto uniform of precisely tailored skirt suits, each paired with some sort of coordinating fascinator tilted just so above her brow. She has selected a soft pink pleated skirt suit from Valentino complete with a ruffled collar, gloves, and a butterfly-trimmed chapeau; a sharp-shouldered version in a deep, almost reptilian satin green from Demna’s Gucci; and a floral, bow-embellished one from Vivienne Westwood. The looks are decidedly feminine in a manner that is at once both futuristic and retro in very Capitol fashion.

Fanning’s stylist Samantha McMillen has been vocal about the references to the character, with captions like “Gucci Effie | @demna puts “The Hunger” into @thehungergames,” and “Elle bringing 🦋 “Sunrise” 🦋 to the Academy Gala.” And it’s not just Fanning doing this either. Costar Mckenna Grace recently recreated a Katniss Everdeen Capitol tour look in which Katniss’s dress quite literally caught fire. Created by Vietnamese designer Cong Tri, who is known for his sculptural silhouettes, the recreation nixed the pyrotechnics but printed graphic flames onto the skirt and bodice. With the help of stylist Molly Dickson, Grace also revived Katniss’s wedding dress with a frothy white, silver-embellished gown by Monique Lhuillier.

I haven’t seen the most recent films, but all of them examine the same concept of this dystopian society propped up by the Hunger Games themselves at different points on a timeline. In the first film specifically, the older Trinket uses their version of a press tour to craft a narrative around Katniss and her fellow tribute Peeta Mellark and win favor among their audience through larger-than-life fashion. The aforementioned fire dress referenced Everdeen’s district’s coal mining industry, and the wedding dress was meant to create the illusion of a love story between the two of them. The series of looks is designed to be projected to the surrounding communities—and garner as much attention as possible. The characters can quite literally benefit later in the life-and-death-style games from the loyalties and sympathies the theatrics spawn.

It’s only in recent years that this idea of method dressing, or theming press tour looks around an actor’s current project, has become commonplace. It began in earnest with the work of Law Roach and Zendaya around the Dune and Spider-Man franchises, Challengers, and The Drama. It was perhaps commercially crystallized in 2023 by Andrew Mukamal and Margot Robbie with Barbie—a press tour that spawned so much digital attention it earned its own coffee-table book—and, more recently, Wuthering Heights. Now, it has proof of concept, attracting stars like Cynthia Erivo and Ariana Grande in their promotion of Wicked, the new cast of Sense and Sensibility, and Lili Reinhart’s rom-com references for The Love Hypothesis. At its most reductive, the tactic is a surefire way to drum up conversation on social media; at its best, a clever way to add depth to a project’s storytelling.

As press tours become more and more oriented towards social media, it’s difficult to isolate the influence of method dressing on the red carpet’s current proclivity towards extremes. Everyone is trying to capitalize on the sort of hype that the Met Gala ensembles can drum up. Your reference needs to be loud enough to cut through the noise—pyrotechnics optional. It’s perhaps why avant-garde, couture, and couture-level designers like Wun, who dressed a large handful of stars at this year’s Met Gala, are carving out more and more space among these sorts of promotional events. At the same time, brands like Standing Ground and Matieres Fecales—it’s only a matter of time before Fanning wears one of those skirt suits—are also gaining traction as stylists treat the red carpet more like a magazine editorial than an occasion to get dressed up.

Then last night, Grace added another layer. She and Dickson have begun to look outside the films to reference looks of Jennifer Lawrence’s own. Lawrence played Katniss in the first movies. For a Madrid premiere, she wore a Miss Sohee couture dress that paid homage to the Givenchy naked dress Lawrence wore for this year’s Golden Globes. It all feels very meta, calling into question the role of fashion in these promotional spaces, real or fictional. On the IRL Hunger Games red carpet, the reference is dystopian extravagance—but with the rise of method dressing and the corresponding celebration of larger, louder looks, has dystopian extravagance already become the norm? In this case, it may not be life and death, but it could make or break your movie.

OpenAI reportedly raises an additional $11 billion at a $1.25 trillion valuation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

OpenAI has reportedly raised $11 billion so far in a new funding round that values the AI lab at $1.25 trillion.

Bloomberg reported on the new funding on Friday, citing sources familiar with the matter. The $11 billion is part of a larger funding round that hasn’t closed yet, according to the outlet. The round is expected to close in the coming weeks and could total about $20 billion, a source told Bloomberg.

In late September, Bloomberg reported that OpenAI was looking to raise $30 billion in the upcoming round. The current figures fall well short of that target.

TechCrunch has reached out to OpenAI for comment and will update this story if it responds.

The news comes only hours after Bloomberg also reported that potential buyers of shares in the IPO of OpenAI’s chief competitor, Anthropic, are eyeing a substantially lower valuation than Anthropic had targeted. Bloomberg writes that some potential buyers believe Anthropic’s valuation could be as low as $1.1 trillion, compared with the $2 trillion that investors were said to be expecting roughly a month ago.

OpenAI previously intimated that it would go public this year but subsequently delayed its plans. There are expectations that the company will IPO next year at some point.

The market for AI hangs in a particularly precarious place. The Democratic landslide in the U.S. midterms — and overwhelmingly negative voter sentiment around data centers — has caused jitters throughout the market, with many anticipating potential regulatory challenges in the future.

UTA, FaZe vets launch ISO as a “new partner for talent”

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A new talent firm is launching with a stacked roster of luminaries from the worlds of streaming, social media, and content creation. Mike Lee and Darren Yan are the leaders of ISO, which is kicking off with a roster of 90 top creators.

ISO (the name stands for “in search of”) is a wide-reaching management firm that touch categories like beauty, food, and art, but its roots are deepest in the gaming and streamer communities. Lee, who will serve as ISO’s CEO, was previously the Head of Gaming and Livestreaming at UTA Creators. Yan, ISO’s Head of Music, is the former Chief Talent Officer of FaZe Clan, where he worked to raise up some of the hottest talents on platforms like Twitch.

At ISO, Lee and Yan have assembled a deep roster that includes big names like PlaqueBoyMax, Jasontheween, Ironmouse, Lacy, Vanillamace, Kyedae, jessechrisss, Angelo Marasigan, and RDCWorld. Some of those creators, like PlaqueBoyMax and Lacy, were previously members of FaZe Clan. Others, like Ironmouse and Vanillamace, have history with UTA.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Influence Media Partners is backing ISO as the new firm takes shape. “The next household name is already online, building an audience, and ISO was built for that talent. Today’s gatekeepers are algorithms, so understanding discovery, engagement and how content travels is essential,” Lee said in a statement. “Every creator we work with is running a real business, and our job is to give it room to grow through original IP, products, live events, and expansion into China and platforms like TikTok Shop, where most of the industry still isn’t set up to compete.”

Lee is serious about ISO’s inroads into East Asia. A press release highlighted internation talent like Vietnam’s Son-Tung MTP and China’s Xin Liu, both of whom are part of the ISO equation.

ISO also plans to innovate on the brand-facing side of its business. Its core team has experience at companies like the NFL, Red Bull, and Fanatics, which are on board as talent partners. Matt Alsberg, ISO’s Head of Talent, previously held roles at PlayStation and Twitch.

With a wealth of ideas and an abundance of talent up and down its roster, ISO is ready to become a management company with deep knowledge of the creator ecosystem. The creators working with ISO are known for their brash, bold-faced personalities — and that attitude seems to fit the firm to a tee.

Godzilla Minus Zero's King Ghidorah Has One Scene That Was 55 Years In The Making

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This article contains minor spoilers for "Godzilla Minus Zero."

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is here, with director Takashi Yamazaki once again getting to put his stamp on the King of the Monsters. He also got to offer up his take on another classic kaiju this time around in the form of King Ghidorah. The monster's appearance was a poorly kept secret, with Ghidorah's design in "Godzilla Minus Zero" leaking thanks to merchandise. But in any event, the monster's inclusion helped resolve a 55-year-old dream for the filmmaker.

According to Reuters, Yamazaki grew up in the Japanese ‌city of Matsumoto. A big "Godzilla" fan, he was particularly excited about 1964's "Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster," one of the best "Godzilla" movies of all time. In particular, he hoped to see the kaiju wreck shop in his hometown. Unfortunately, Matsumoto only appeared fleetingly in the movie after Ghidorah's emergence.

Given that "Godzilla Minus One" became the highest-grossing Japanese Godzilla movie ever en route to winning an Oscar, Yamazaki was given a lot of creative freedom on the sequel. He used that freedom to show Ghidorah doing what he wanted to see as a child. "Minus Zero" prominently features Matsumoto, with Ghidorah carving a path of destruction there relatively early on in the movie's runtime. Here's what the filmmaker had to say about it:

"55 years later, I had King ‌Ghidorah ​appear in my own film. I had the chance ⁠to make the movie I ⁠wanted to watch back then, and I created the footage that I wanted to show my younger self, as if to say, 'This is what you wanted to see, isn't it?'"

"So, my dream has finally come true after ​55 years, and I am truly happy," Takashi Yamazaki concluded.

The sequel picks up two years after the events of "Minus One" as Japan is still trying to put itself back together in the aftermath of Godzilla's attacks. Things are once again thrown into chaos when a new, mysterious, dragon-like monster emerges. It's not long before Godzilla is back as well, leaving the people of Japan scrambling to figure out how to deal with not one but two kaiju.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is rated R, which meant that Yamazaki didn't have to hold back at all. When it came to having King Ghidorah destroy his hometown, he certainly made the most of that R rating. In a bizarre method of showing pride for the place he came from, he made the most of Matsumoto in his latest cinematic effort. Being from Japan and having your home shown off in a "Godzilla" movie is, understandably, something to be proud of.

King Ghidorah was the movie's "surprise" villain who has a long history with the franchise. "Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster" was his first of many, many appearances across the many eras of this franchise. In recent years, he's been showcased in everything from "Godzilla: King of the Monsters" in the MonsterVerse to the animated "Godzilla: The Planet Eater" on Netflix. He's without a doubt Godzilla's most notorious foe.

"Godzilla Minus Zero" is in theaters now.

Milania Giudice 'Did Not Leave' BFF Victoria Zardoya's Side in the Hospital

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Milania Giudice stayed by her best friend Victoria Zardoya’s side in the days before her death.

“I just know Milania has been through a lot,” Real Housewives of New Jersey star Jennifer Aydin told The Daily Mail in an interview published on Friday, November 6. “She lost her best friend. Her best friend was an organ donor. When you are an organ donor, you have to wait for them to come and retrieve your organs.”

She continued, “So you will be in the bed for sometimes days, maybe even over a week. And Milania did not leave her best friend’s side … She was in the hospital with her until they retrieved her organs. She did not leave her side, and Teresa flew to Tampa to support Milania and Victoria’s family. And that’s a lot for anybody to handle, let alone, you know, a young adult. So, she’s had a lot thrown her way.”

Aydin, 49 — a close friend of RHONJ costar and Milania’s mom, Teresa Giudice — said that Milania, 20, was at the hospital with Zardoya for “at least five days or more.”

Zardoya, Milania’s childhood best friend, died at age 20 in July after suffering an accidental fall while exploring the ruins of Fort Dade from the Spanish-American War during a trip to Egmont Key, an island off the coast of Tampa Bay, Florida.

According to the autopsy report obtained by Us on Tuesday, November 3, Zardoya’s cause of death was listed as “craniocerebral trauma,” including skull fractures. The manner of death is listed as “accident (fall from height and injured self).”

According to the National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke, craniocerebral trauma, or traumatic brain injury (TBI), is damage to the brain caused by an external force, commonly categorized into penetrating and non-penetrating injuries. It results from events like falls, crashes or blows to the head.

Toxicology test results determined that Zardoya was positive for ketamine and norketamine, a substance produced when the body breaks down the drug. The results also showed alcohol and etomidate, a medication used for sedation.

A postmortem examination from the Sarasota, Florida, medical examiner’s office confirmed Zardoya was an organ donor and donated both of her lungs, heart, liver, both kidneys and more following her death.

Milania has been open about her struggles with grief following Victoria’s death.

“If this summer taught me anything, it is to never judge anybody no matter what you see, what you hear. Never judge anybody. It also taught me that life is all about sacrifice,” she said in an Instagram video last month after she made headlines for a tense exchange with her mom at the Tampa International Airport.

Milania continued, “Empathy is a trait that I’ve personally have always had. When I love, I want to move mountains for you. When you’re upset, I’m upset. When you’re angry, I’m angry. I’m a ride-or-die friend. I’m ride or die for my family.”

Milania entered a treatment center last month after she was diagnosed with bipolar disorder, depression, anxiety, PTSD and ADHD.

“I am not ashamed of any of my mental health disorders because I’m currently in a treatment center,” she said in a video from the center. “My mom’s actually on her way to come visit me today, and I do work on myself. But you know what? I am only 20 years old, and this summer hit me like a bus.”

Teresa, 54, has also shown her support for Milania in the wake of Zardoya’s death.

“As a mother, there is nothing more important to me than the health, safety, and well-being of my children,” she said in a statement in September. “The past few months have been incredibly difficult for our family, and I feel it is important to say something while also protecting my daughter during an extremely vulnerable time in her life.”

Teresa added, “Grief affects everyone differently, and watching your child try to process that kind of loss is something no parent can ever truly prepare for. Right now, my only focus is my daughter — making sure she is surrounded by love, family, support, and the care she needs.”

5 Fall Trends Designers Think Will Still Look Good Next Spring

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Above: A hallway view of Eric Egan’s Milan apartment.

If any season has the power to inspire a complete aesthetic overhaul, it’s fall. Nature’s changing palette inspires us to follow suit, as each year our mood boards make new space for jewel tones, cozy textures, and a bottomless candle budget. Autumnal trends are so alluring, it almost feels like a risky time to make long-term design decisions. But with the right consideration, that seasonal inspiration can translate into choices that look good year-round.

This fall in particular, designers are urging clients to trust their guts—making sure they see themselves in the spaces they create, rather than blindly following the latest fads. “For me, a trend has to be authentic or it’s just noise,” says designer Chad Dorsey, who’s eager to ditch the spare minimalism of years past for looks with more personality. ELLE Decor A-Lister Kerry Joyce seconds the motion: “By prioritizing personalized storytelling, a space becomes evergreen, evolving as smaller objects are swapped out over time.”

But an expert nudge in the right direction never hurts. This fall, top designers are gravitating toward creative color-drenching, cleverly concealed storage, tactile upholstery, and rooms built around art. Read on for their takes on the fall 2026 trends worth investing in, plus tips on building (as Dorsey puts it) “spaces designed to be lived in, not looked at.”

Fabric and upholstery are trending ultra plush and textured for fall, but when thoughtfully integrated, they feel more timeless than seasonal. “Design is shifting toward how a space feels, not just how it looks,” says Joyce, who’s seeing an increased popularity in fabrics like mohair, velvet, cashmere, and alpaca hair. “This focus on texture does more than elevate a room's aesthetic,” he explains. “It creates a sensory experience that comforts the soul.”

Start your bedroom redesign this fall and your space will strike the perfect balance between fresh and lived-in by spring. Between client requests and a personal home build, Amber Lewis has bedrooms on the brain right now. One trend takes the lead in her eyes: “I love matching the walls to the bed upholstery and bedding so the whole room feels wrapped in one warm color family. It’s calm and peaceful, and that enveloping feeling works in any season.”

Luckily, this cozy trend doesn’t require a complete room overhaul: “The easiest place to start is the bed itself. Reupholster your headboard in a pattern you love, or drape a vintage suzani across the foot of the bed,” Lewis says. “One move like that changes the whole mood of the room without starting over.” To bring in a little dimension and pop, she recommends adding a singular statement, like a lone striped pillow.

If your primary suite needs a more holistic update, Lewis recommends creating a small sitting area. Sourcing vintage chairs and small sofas for primary suites this fall has given Lewis a new perspective on where bedroom design is headed: “True sanctuaries that feel layered, personal, and built for actually living in.”

Color drenching isn’t going anywhere for fall 2026—or long after, according to Dorsey. When choosing a shade for a room, he finds that any color works harder when it’s layered and intentional. Color capping—a term coined by Benjamin Moore referring to painting a room in multiple tones from one color family—can help keep a space from feeling flat or overwhelmed beneath a statement hue.

According to Dorsey, the staying power of color drenching goes hand in hand with the shift away from spareness and toward “collected” spaces: “Neither is really a ‘look;’ they’re about living with things you love, which is why they’ll carry well into spring.”

Designer Regan Baker says that cleverly hidden space solutions are also on the rise. “It could be a TV that slides behind a commissioned art panel, a Murphy bed tucked into a wall of millwork or that serves as a desk when it's stored, or a peg wall that’s a place to hang a jacket one minute and a standing desk the next,” she says. These solutions are tailored to her clients’ spaces (rather than tied to a season or palette), which gives them staying power.

This concept is an easy way to turn the corner you’ve never known how to style into your favorite part of the room. “In an existing home, look at the awkward spots, like an alcove or the wall around a fireplace, and treat built-ins there as a design moment instead of an afterthought,” Baker advises.

If there’s one built-to-last fall trend most designers can agree on, it’s added eccentricity. “Homes are steering away from the sterile, overnight look of a showroom delivery,” Joyce says. “The defining philosophy now focuses on layering modern staples with antiques, secondhand finds, travel mementos, and new pieces that carry a sense of patina.”

Dorsey describes this style as “collected rather than prescribed,” where a high-low mix of vintage and custom elements gel together. And while it may feel loftier than a trending, sold-out piece, Baker says that most clients start with an artistic anchor, then build around it: “We’re designing more homes around a piece of art than around a color trend. That might be wallpaper by local designers, a commissioned piece, or even a palette pulled from a lichen-covered rock found on the property.”

One key takeaway? Curate like a collector. Your reward may be a space that never goes out of style.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

Starlink Mobile: All the news on SpaceX&#8217;s major mobile carrier ambitions

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

SpaceX has declared that it’s going to make Starlink Mobile a “major” mobile carrier. The Elon Musk-owned company has already deployed thousands of direct-to-cell satellites, but its next big challenge is building out its 5G network here on Earth.

As part of its plans to rival AT&T, Verizon, and T-Mobile, SpaceX has inked a deal with Qualcomm, which will deploy its cellular technology across tens of thousands of 5G cell sites starting in 2027. SpaceX is also reportedly in talks to buy Boost Mobile, potentially allowing it to launch Starlink Mobile through the service. Another report suggests SpaceX may tap into 80,000 cell sites across the US to build out its network.

SpaceX’s big plans have garnered some reactions in the telecom industry. Tim Höttges, the CEO of T-Mobile’s parent company Deutsche Telekom, said he takes SpaceX’s plans “super serious” but that it could cost Musk more than $200 billion to pull off.

Follow along for the latest updates on Starlink’s major mobile carrier ambitions.

Today, I’m talking with Walter Piecyk, a partner and analyst at LightShed Partners who covers tech, media, and telecom, about SpaceX’s recently announced plan to become a major wireless carrier.

Last week, SpaceX agreed to buy a big chunk of low-band spectrum to expand the Starlink network, spectrum that’s been traded around the telecom business for decades now. SpaceX’s ambitious plan is to use that spectrum to help build “the world’s most advanced mobile network.”

Bloomberg has a breakdown of how Starlink Mobile will operate — and what SpaceX still needs to do to get its hybrid 5G network up and running.

SpaceX is teaming up with Qualcomm to build out its nationwide mobile network. Under the multi-year deal, SpaceX will bring Qualcomm’s Dragonwing technology to “tens of thousands” of 5G cell sites across the US, with the first deployments set for 2027.

The news of the partnership comes as part of SpaceX’s push to make Starlink Mobile a “major” carrier in the US, competing directly with T-Mobile, AT&T, and Verizon. The Elon Musk-owned company has already snapped up a swath of spectrum, while recent reports suggested that SpaceX was exploring an acquisition of Boost Mobile.

SpaceX has discussed a deal with Boost Mobile to launch its own service through the budget-friendly carrier, according to a report from The Washington Post. The deal, which comes as SpaceX aims to make Starlink a “major” mobile carrier, reportedly involves the Elon Musk-owned company acquiring Boost Mobile or accessing its network.

Musk discussed the potential agreement with Charlie Ergen, chairman of Boost Mobile parent company EchoStar, last month, as reported by The Washington Post. Boost Mobile’s network is currently powered by T-Mobile and AT&T, meaning SpaceX may need permission from the two carriers to launch its service.

SpaceX plans to access at least 80,000 cell sites as part of efforts to compete directly with Verizon, T-Mobile, and AT&T, according to a report from Bloomberg. Sources tell the publication that SpaceX is in talks with Crown Castle Inc., one of the nation’s largest cell tower owners.

The move would give SpaceX access to half of the 158,500 cell towers currently operational in the US, according to Reuters. When asked about a potential partnership with SpaceX, Amazon, or other satellite services during an earnings call on Wednesday, Crown Castle CEO Chris Hillabrant said, “I don’t think I want to get into customer-specific detail, but you can assume that we’re talking to all parties.” SpaceX plans to pair a network of cell towers with its constellation of direct-to-cell satellites.

SpaceX has acquired a portfolio of low-band spectrum licenses — a move the company says “will pave the way” for its Starlink Mobile service to become a “major” US carrier. When the Federal Communications Commission signs off on the deal, SpaceX says it will deploy its new architecture that combines its satellite-to-mobile constellation with a terrestrial mobile network, directly competing with T-Mobile, AT&T, and Verizon.

The licenses include up to 14 megahertz of paired spectrum in the 800 MHz band, which SpaceX says will allow Starlink Mobile’s signal to penetrate through walls and buildings. SpaceX previously acquired 2GHz spectrum from EchoStar as part of efforts to bolster its direct-to-device service.

“The spectrum that we purchased from EchoStar does have terrestrial components, so we definitely intend to build out the terrestrial component,” said Shotwell. “You will have not only the capacity from the satellites themselves, but you will have a buildout of the terrestrial, basically the hardware and systems necessary to make a true mobile service exactly what you want it to be.”

Ships loiter near subsea cables amid Iran's warnings to Trump

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Iran threatened to attack subsea cables if US escalates military strikes again.

Several tanker ships linked to Iran have been loitering over subsea Internet cables in the Gulf of Oman near the Strait of Hormuz—a possible indication that the Iranian government is preparing to attack such undersea infrastructure in response to any renewed US and Israeli strikes.

The movements of three sanctioned tankers and an Iranian fishing boat have been tracked near major undersea cables by the maritime intelligence firm Windward in October, according to the Financial Times. Six active cables currently cross through the contested Strait of Hormuz, linking the Middle East to Europe, Africa and Asia, with two more cables having segments under construction in the region.

Four countries, including Iraq, Kuwait, Bahrain and Qatar, depend entirely upon such subsea telecommunications connections running through the Strait of Hormuz chokepoint to link with the outside world. But other countries also depend heavily on the region’s undersea cable network—one third of India’s westbound Internet traffic goes through the Persian Gulf and Strait of Hormuz.

One cable under construction, the 2Africa cable, is owned by a tech consortium that includes the US company Meta. That was one of the major cable corridors visited by the drifting Iran-linked ships.

Windward used both ship-tracking data and satellite images to track the vessels as they slowed down and looped around near multiple cables, with some ships then “going dark” by switching off their automatic identification system (AIS) transponders that normally broadcast their location and other information.

Such movement and behavioral patterns suggest the Iran-linked ships may be “mapping the cable routes, or placing charges or mines on them while dark,” Windward told the Financial Times.

Windward highlighted the examples of two sanctioned tankers linked to Iran that spent days drifting “directly over regional cable corridors… both with no commercial reason to stop” in a blog post on October 26. Both ships carried no commercial cargo.

Since the US and Israel first launched a major war with Iran in February 2026, Iran’s response beyond targeting US military bases, ships and aircraft has included attacking commercial shipping in the Strait of Hormuz and hitting oil refineries and other energy infrastructure along with airports in various Gulf countries.

Iran has also made a point of threatening or attacking US Big Tech infrastructure in the Middle East, including launching multiple drone strikes on Amazon data centers. In May 2026, a spokesperson for Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps claimed Iran would “impose fees on Internet cables” belonging to US tech companies and other owners.

But more recently, multiple Iranian officials and commanders of Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) have warned that they could directly attack the undersea cables running through the Strait of Hormuz. This came after President Donald Trump repeatedly threatened to renew major US military strikes on Iran and warned he could “annihilate” the country during a speech at the United Nations in New York on September 22.

“Seven major cables run through the Strait of Hormuz, supporting global Internet operations and commercial data transmission,” one IRGC spokesperson said on October 20. “We remind the enemy of this.”

The latest phase of the war has involved more limited US military strikes against Iranian targets, even as Trump has alternated between threats and touting the possibility of a diplomatic breakthrough. Meanwhile, Iran has increased drone strikes and other attacks on commercial ships to exert its own pressure on the United States, with October marking the highest number of ship attacks since the war began.

Much damage has already been done. The limited fleet of cable-laying ships used for installation and repair has not been able to venture safely into the region since the war began, which forced the Meta-backed 2Africa cable to pause its planned expansion. The war has also compelled US tech companies and Gulf countries to accelerate efforts to develop alternative cable routes bypassing the Strait of Hormuz.

The Middle East is not the only part of the world where threats to the Internet’s undersea backbone have intensified. Subsea infrastructure is increasingly at risk from sabotage and gray-zone warfare from the Baltic Sea to the Pacific Ocean.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Some are calling this 'Tuchel 2.0' and his latest squad does feel like that. Here's why England think a tilt at the European Championship is within reach, writes IAN LADYMAN

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:35 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 17:39 EDT, 6 November 2026

One of Thomas Tuchel’s favourite analogies when it comes to the development of his England team is that of a train leaving a station with only the chosen few on it. As time moves on, the bodies lying on the tracks are growing greater in number.

Consider this statistic. Of the 26 players who travelled to the World Cup in America 13 are not in the squad chosen by England manager Tuchel for next week’s Nations League games against Croatia and Spain. And that does not include Cole Palmer and Phil Foden who, after missing out in the summer, were expected by many of us to have forced a way back in by now.

Tuchel’s latest selection has been affected by injuries, of course. But more significant is that the experienced German believes he is now starting to see an England team and squad develop in a way and in a direction that even he hadn’t envisaged once Argentina ended his World Cup hopes in that dismal Atlanta semi-final in July.

Different feelings, different style of play and, to a degree, different players.

Some are calling this ‘Tuchel 2.0’ and it does feel a little bit like that. The start of the second phase of Tuchel’s tenure, the start of the road towards a tilt at another trophy, the European Championships of 2028.

Having admitted to some worry ahead of the four-game international break FIFA saddled us all with last month, Tuchel sat down yesterday looking and sounding like a man once again certain of himself.

Tuchel sat down with the media looking and sounding like a man once again certain of himself

The inclusion of Jobe Bellingham — Jude’s younger brother (right) — was the headline act but there were other messages written within

Certain enough of a direct, quick, athletic style of play that helped England to two wins over Czech Republic and one – by seven goals – against Croatia in Rijeka. And certain that a dependence on players like Jordan Henderson, Dan Burn and John Stones for what we may politely call ‘squad culture management’ lies now in the past.

All of this may crash down in Madrid next Sunday. Spain have already beaten Tuchel’s England once – at Wembley in late September – and a repeat this month would ask familiar questions about England against the really good teams.

But for now, Tuchel is confident enough to claim that he has found his path.

‘We said at some point we need to take the next step, to build on what we have, to get better and to improve otherwise you’re just left behind,’ said Tuchel yesterday.

‘That’s how football works. You have been champion in one season but you need to improve, you need to bring in new players for competition, young players, new energy, fresh energy. It’s also the way, naturally, the national team goes.

‘We cannot be stuck in the same personnel and in the same style of play.

‘I agreed with you last month. I was the first to admit I was not 100 per cent happy with the pure level of football we played in the US. I saw a higher level in training [than on the field], I saw a higher level in glimpses.

‘We adjusted some things tactically, created some new positions and took the next steps.’

The Tuchel we sat down with in late September still wore the scars of the summer’s disappointment – and the criticism that came with it – rather visibly. It was the first time we had seen him like that in his two years in the job. He was tetchy and easily provoked. Call it the Argentina effect if you like.

Here, he appeared rather healed. Good results tend to do that for a manager and a little time helps too. If he believes his team has moved on then maybe he has also and he had to.

Certainly, his selection squad speaks to that. The inclusion of Jobe Bellingham – Jude’s younger brother – was the headline act but there were other messages written within.

Henderson and Burn are available but not included. So too Arsenal’s Noni Madueke, excluded to the benefit of the more direct Liverpool winger Rio Ngumoha.

‘We need runners who will go between the lines,’ said Tuchel. A clear indication, there, that too much of England’s summer wing play was too slow and too conservatively narrow.

Palmer, Foden and Kobbie Mainoo are also on the outside looking in once again and the case of the Manchester United midfielder is perhaps indicative of how Tuchel now operates.

Moved to apologise to him in front of the whole squad in July for his lack of minutes at the World Cup, Tuchel was determined Mainoo would play in the opening four games of the Nations League campaign.

‘I was desperate to give him the minutes and desperate to include him in this new start,’ explained Tuchel.

‘But then he pulled out. He missed an excellent camp and also entered a difficult, difficult spell for himself.

‘Man United fell off a cliff a little bit performance-wise. So this is why he now suffers.’

We can talk about the whys and wherefores of that logic. If Mainoo was good enough in September then why is he not good enough now? How desperate could Tuchel really have been to play him that one withdrawal with injury closed the door for the next camp and opened it instead for Bellingham jnr?

This, though, is how Tuchel works. He is an adept tactician but he’s also a mood coach, someone who – after sobering club experiences at places like PSG, Chelsea and Bayern Munich – places huge emphasis on environments and gut feels and culture. If he feels something works, he doesn’t like to pick it apart without good reason.

This is why for example the growing voice and influence of Elliot Anderson has left Jordan Henderson suddenly surplus. Tuchel says the door is not closed on a player who will be 38 by the time the next Euros are played and he called him this week to say so. In Tuchel’s mind, though, England will now move without the veteran and others of his ilk.

‘I was concerned before the last camp that we missed the players who kept our standards,’ said Tuchel.

‘I was worried that we missed so many of them. But then this group found their own way and their own voices.

It’s also reasonable to ask why our head coach can’t find room in his squad – if not his team – for the game changing duo of Palmer and Foden

‘I know what the others did. They built a platform on which these ones can build.’

Time will tell. To a degree, Spain will tell. Can England play the kind of higher tempo, front foot football we saw against Croatia and the Czechs against the world and European champions next Sunday? They embarrassed us when we didn’t have the ball at Wembley.

It’s also reasonable to ask why our head coach can’t find room in his squad – if not his team – for the game-changing duo of Palmer and Foden. England lacked good substitutes at the World Cup. There was too much like for like.

England need to win in order for such decisions to be vindicated and Tuchel knows that.

Asked yesterday if he felt as invigorated as he looked he said he did. ‘The last camp gave me a lot of energy, gave me a lot of extra belief,’ he said.

‘It gave me a lot of optimism to not compromise on the way England teams should play. Be physical but also step up in the pure level of performances.’

It’s certainly time for this now. The summer only served to expose English shortcomings. Tuchel’s train must arrive in Madrid next weekend, not only with fresh personnel but also new ideas clearly apparent.

The Cybertruck’s Death Is Actually Great News for EVs

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tesla will end production of the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory next year.

It’s a uniquely toxic electric vehicle, between its divisive design and Elon Musk’s embrace of you name it. That toxicity certainly contributed to subpar sales, along with a number of recalls.

But the end of the Cybertruck is also another sign that the era of big EVs—particularly big trucks—is waning. That’s great news for pedestrian and biker safety, but also for the battery supply chain at a time when more people need to get behind the electric wheel.

The Cybertruck uses a massive 123-kilowatt-hour battery that clocks in at around 1,600 pounds, or around a quarter of the vehicle's weight. That’s on par with the battery that powered Ford’s F-150 Lightning, another electric truck that was canceled late last year. (The company also killed a three-row EV in 2024.) Ford’s behemoth batteries ranged from 98 kilowatt-hours up to 131 kilowatt-hours.

Seeing both trucks canceled within a year of each other, while automakers have announced a bevy of smaller EVs and gas-powered autos with electric motors that can extend range, indicates there may be a shift afoot. Ford is planning to launch the Fathom next year. The sub-$30,000 electric pickup is expected to be about the size of the automaker’s compact gas-powered Maverick pickup. Though Ford hasn’t announced the battery sizes available in various trims, it’s a safe bet they won’t be anywhere near the size of the F-150 Lightning's or Cybertruck's. The company is also planning to relaunch the Lightning as an extended-range model.

Startup Slate is also launching a tiny electric pickup that can be converted to an SUV, with a bare-bones base model starting below $25,000. It also features a svelte battery and has seen a massive surge in preorders—though, to be fair, so did the Cybertruck, and we saw how that turned out.

Then there’s the Rivian R2, which launched this year. While the SUV isn’t as tiny or cheap as Ford and Slate’s vehicles—its battery has 88 kilowatt-hours of capacity and weighs in at around 1,200 pounds—it’s still smaller than the company’s flagship R1S. Rivian has also teased the R3, an even more-slimmed down SUV, in the coming years.

That’s not to say big EVs are dead. The Kia EV9, for example, is among the top 10 EVs by sales in the US, as is the R1S. There’s also a torrid resale market for the F-150 Lightning. But the rest of the top 10 new EV sales are all crossovers, sedans, or mid-sized SUVs with moderately sized batteries, and a number of newly announced EVs are trending on the smaller side or function as extended-range vehicles.

Building smaller vehicles is, of course, one way to make EVs cheaper. With less weight to propel, you can rely on a smaller battery. And given that the battery pack is the most costly component of an EV, that can in turn help automakers offer more affordable vehicles at a time when the price of so many other consumer goods is skyrocketing. That includes the current crop of EVs: The average transaction price for a new model is now $55,800, according to Cox Automotive.

Going small—or smaller, anyway—also means making the most of a limited battery supply chain. The vast majority of Cybertruck drivers are doing the same thing with their vehicles as Hyundai Ioniq 5 drivers: getting groceries, commuting, and driving kids to soccer games. But a standard-range Ioniq 5 driver is doing it with a battery about half the size.

After President Donald Trump’s administration killed EV incentives last year, sales plummeted. The Iran War and rising gas prices have led to an uptick in interest, though sales still lag behind the global average. (Used hybrid sales have also picked up, reflecting the pain people have been feeling at the pump.) Even with a Democratic-controlled Congress, EV incentives coming back under Trump are about as likely as defense secretary Pete Hegseth putting pronouns in his email signature. But should Democrats take the White House in 2028, marking the culmination of Woke 2.0, support for would-be EV buyers becomes much more likely.

Automakers pivoting to smaller cars could be much better positioned to meet pent-up demand if that happens, while also lowering the risks of delivery delays.

Even Elon’s announcement killing the Cybertruck points to where the market is heading: He claims Tesla is ending production to make room for cranking out more Cybercabs. The battery size in those vehicles? Just 48 kilowatt-hours.

Kasper Hogh riddle continues to fox Celtic as Martin O'Neill opens up on misfiring striker

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

“Kasper had a brilliant game. That's the life of a striker - you have to be there to take them. His all-round game today was very good. He gave us a platform to play.”

Sunday, August 9, the words of Celtic assistant coach Shaun Maloney after watching striker Kasper Hogh score a 33-minute hat-trick in a 5-1 win over Kilmarnock.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Hogh had not long arrived at Celtic from Bodo/Glimt for £12 million, a record signing for the Scottish champions. His predatory instincts in Ayrshire led many to believe the Dane was on his way to the golden boot.

Fifteen games on for Celtic, Hogh has not scored a goal since. In fact, his manager Martin O’Neill is yet to see him find the net, as he was recovering from a “small procedure” for the match at Rugby Park.

Maybe the visit of Kilmarnock, who he terrorised on that summer’s afternoon, on Sunday will be the match where Hogh finally finds the net again. That is, of course, if the 25-year-old gets the nod in attack.

Hogh’s star is waning. The 25-year-old has been eclipsed by fellow July signing Camilo Duran, who has netted 13 times this season and clambered off the bench to help Celtic defeat Besiktas on Thursday night. The Colombian did more in his half-hour cameo than Hogh did in double that time. The poor hitman didn’t touch the ball once in the Turkish penalty box in the opening period.

It would be unfair to judge Hogh wholly on his goals, although at £4m per strike so far, that is an expensive outlay. Physical and hard-working, he offers Celtic a focal point. O’Neill was pleased with his performance against Besiktas. “I want centre-forwards to be able to hold it and that is what he did,” said the manager. “He is not Duran, he is not a dribbler. But if he can do this for us against strong opposition it will stand him in good stead.”

Hogh’s belief is bound to be low. How could it not be? He is out of the Denmark squad for the first time in over a year and when he tapped in from an already rescinded free-kick against Besiktas, there was a slightly sarcastic cheer from the home crowd. The misfiring forward is a real talking point.

“I think Thursday night will be good for him,” continued O’Neill. “I said to him that I’m not worried about whether he scores a goal. Of course you want centre-forwards to score and of course centre-forwards live on them. But I said to him that last night I wanted him to be strong and fight when the ball is played up to him. I thought in terms of holding the ball up and bringing people into the game that he did really well.

“Centre-forwards need a goal. But that performance there against European opposition will give him confidence. I don’t know what side we will play him on Sunday. But this lad has scored goals in the Champions League, remember. He will get a confidence boost from last night in terms of doing that part of the game well. But it will be nice for him to score a goal.”

The problem Hogh perhaps had is that O’Neill will turn to Duran more often than not now. “At this minute, he is on fire,” added O’Neill of the South American. “He can play wide right, he has done that. He has played wide left. But centre forward is where he is dynamic.” For now, unlike his strike partner.

With the remake out on Switch 2 now, it would have been easy for Nintendo to scrub some of that stuff out, but as some players have found, some of the funnier opportunities for player-driven silliness are still included in the game, including the fact that the Iron Boots can ruin serious cutscenes.

The Iron Boots are a piece of equipment Link can wear to walk under the surface of water, allowing him to reach places he wouldnât be able to swim to. Theyâre often paired with the Zora Tunic, which allows Link to breathe indefinitely, and these two items together are crucial for places like the Water Temple which has Link going in and out of fully submerged areas. However, because these Iron Boots weigh Link down, they also let out clanking sounds as he tries to walk with them. Still, the game lets you put them on anywhere you want, and if you don them when Link has to walk across an area for a serious moment, youâre gonna hear him coming.

As players are discovering, this quirk is still in the remake, and somehow, the boots are even louder coming through the Switch 2âs speakers.

Vija Celmins, the Ultimate Artist’s Artist, Gets Honored by LACMA

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

On Wednesday, I walked down Crosby Street and stopped at an easy-to-miss building where, high above the cobblestoned street, is the studio of Vija Celmins, who’s been there for almost 50 years. It’s a classic gigantic SoHo artist loft, the kind you’ll be shocked to hear still exist—but they do. In the 1970s, the previous tenant put a Buddhist temple in the front room. Not finding anything particularly wrong with that, Celmins lived in the temple for a while.

“Then I finally thought, gee, maybe I could put a couch in here,” Celmins told me as I walked in.

Read True Colors: Inside the global deals and colorful critiques that make the art world go round, with a weekly guide to the must-see openings, auctions, and fairs.

It’s been decades since the galleries moved from SoHo to Chelsea and got replaced by boutiques. But among this warren of cast iron buildings, some of the artists who moved in during the pioneer days remain. Among them is Celmins, the masterful pop-landscapist who paints in infinite textures, taking on the universal with an obsessive tenacity. The 88-year-old is a living link to a long-gone generation, and someone always in the running when we talk about the best artists who are both still with us and who began their careers before the JFK assassination.

Approaching nonagenarian status, Celmins is still incredibly quick, with a generous, buoyant voice. She immediately hopped down the stairs of her old studio and took me over to the windows.

“I used to be able to see down the street before they built this ugly pink building—all the way to the bridge, because this was a parking lot,” she told me, referring to a building erected 21 years ago.

She walked past a print she made some years ago: a depiction of the ocean, her most famous subject, installed on the wall. She admired it in a refreshingly objective way.

“It’s so loose. Usually I tighten everything down,” she said. “I mean, it’s such a relief. I had forgotten that. And what did I do? I only made 10 of them, which is interesting, in ’16. I must have done this at… It looks like it’s a drypoint—doesn’t it look like drypoint? I mean, I did a fucking lot of oceans, as you know.”

Though not a household name, in certain circles, Celmins has attained near-mythic status. The very few people I told about this interview ahead of time were shocked she was having anyone over to her studios in Manhattan and Long Island. She works incredibly slowly, and thus has shown incredibly infrequently. This has started to change in the last few years. She had a retrospective at the Met Breuer in 2019, and in the last few years, a few shows of new work at her gallery, Matthew Marks—the blue-chip commercial concern that represents a disproportionate number of the greatest American artists.

Next year, Celmins will have a show at Dia Bridgehampton. Tomorrow, she’s the guest of honor at the LACMA Art+Film Gala, a big deal in American museumdom. It comes with a gigantic bash presided over by Leonardo DiCaprio and Eva Chow. The gala’s also honoring Denis Villeneuve, which Celmins is excited about. She’s seen the Dune movies and, like Villeneuve, is a big fan of the desert.

During my visit, she opened up a random drawer under a drawing table. There was stuff everywhere, decades of sketches and notes and Post-its. She has a constellation of postcards taped to the wall, with paintings as inspiration, ones that look nothing like Vija Celmins—Picasso, Balthus—along with photos of her heroes and her friends with pets. There’s Jasper Johns with his dog, Borges with his cat. She’s got framed artworks from friends who are mostly dead now, like a Ken Price drawing of some undulating cups—“You know Kenny Price?”—and a very kooky, absolutely amazing H.C. Westermann of a wolf with its heart throbbing in its chest, wandering through the desert. Westermann made it for her. She made Westermann a wolf drawing in return. She doesn’t know what happened to her wolf.

There are drawings of globes, sculptures of globes, her tablet paintings, more prints of the ocean, a paperweight from an insurance company in Kansas City—near where she grew up in Indiana after leaving Latvia—a wrapped-up canvas labeled “ED MOSES” on the side, and plenty of drawings of Saturn.

She moved into the SoHo place after two decades working in Venice Beach, California. While in LA, she honed and refined, and inarguably mastered, a painstakingly laborious practice of applying graphite to paper in slow, methodical marks, rendering the passage of time in hyperreal landscapes. She now mostly works in a studio on the eastern end of Long Island, where she's had a place for the last few decades. But it’s here at the SoHo studio, in the erstwhile cauldron of cutting-edge American art, where she loves to be.

“The studio out in the country is more like where I went back to painting, really,” she told me. “And here I did all these weird things.”

This wasn’t the first time we had met. A few weeks back, I was admiring the very cool Gary Hume show that Marks had up in his gallery on 24th Street when gallery director Jacqueline Tran tapped me on the shoulder and asked if I would like to meet Vija Celmins. Vija Celmins! At the public opening!

We chatted for a few moments. She seemed spry, fit, with an elastic mind. Then she invited me over for lunch—not in the city, but at her place on farmland out east. On an October morning I hopped on a Jitney, then an Uber to Celmins’s part of the hamlet, which is essentially rural, with small clapboard homes encircled by crop formations.

I arrived at the same time as my fellow visitors: Michael Govan, the director of LACMA, and his wife, the fashion executive Katherine Ross. Celmins welcomed us in and introduced us to her cat, Raymond, who immediately pounced on my cup of takeout coffee.

“Down, Raymond! He’s very friendly,” Celmins said. “He’s my son.”

Out here, in the two-room studio attached to her house, she’s been able to conjure a vision of landscape perhaps more potent than the breakthroughs she came to so many years ago. In her studio, she showed us how the raindrops on her window, as captured in her work-intensive flattened plane panorama, could take on epic qualities.

We sat down for lunch—quiche and soup from a local bakery—and Celmins started telling stories about California. She hadn’t lived there in a number of years, though the topography of the state is what willed her artwork into being. She drove out to the desert with James Turrell, with Doug Wheeler, and spent years—decades—arguing with Robert Irwin, who taught her at UCLA. Irwin called Celmins his “star student.” I’ve always liked this Bob Irwin quote about Celmins: “Vija has perfect taste. She doesn’t like anything.”

Govan told her about the museum, LACMA, and the new building that had finally been completed earlier this year after two decades of planning and fundraising. Celmins was quite curious about it all. She has a few plans for when she’s in town for the LACMA gala. She seemed intrigued about meeting her fellow honoree, director Villeneuve, and watched the Dune movies in preparation. (I heartily recommended she watch Sicario and Arrival as well.) She’s going to visit her old studio at 701 Venice Boulevard, which she hasn’t seen in decades.

“I was going to go out in the desert, but I’m not going to have time to do it,” she said. “I might have to make another trip. I really like the Death Valley desert because it’s so vast, so terrific. And they have airplanes zooming from the Air Force around really fast. I used to wave at them. They go really low, and they just go like, vvvvvrrroooooom! Right above, following the outline of the mountains.”

She’s also going to see Riko Mizuno, her Los Angeles art dealer, who is currently 94 years old. But most of her friends are gone.

“A lot of them have died, I mean, I didn’t have that many friends, but some died, and some are dying right now,” she said. “And then they moved away. Ed Moses, I knew pretty well. Billy Al Bengston. Well, Kenny Price, I loved Kenny.”

Then she mentioned Robert Therrien—“You know Bob Therrien?”—the late artist who has become quite famous for oversized sculptures that are astronomically popular on Instagram. A retrospective at The Broad this year became something of a sensation. I can’t imagine an artist more different than Celmins—but as it turns out, they had a deep mutual respect for each other.

“I met him once, and he was a very unusual person,” Celmins said. “He was really tall, and everything in his studio was like—he had a ceiling that was maybe like 25, 28 feet tall. And he had this little ladder that went up this side to this kitchen, which was this kitchen he remade from Chicago or someplace where his family used to live. I’ll show you one little thing. He gave me this.”

She got up from her chair and found the Therrian artwork on a shelf. It was a snout, bobbing up and down in a terrifying manner, attached to a curved metal rod. Just the inner part of the face of a canine, floating forever.

“It’s so disorienting. Where’s the rest of the dog?” I asked.

Roberto De Zerbi reveals Tottenham right-back conundrum in new Pedro Porro injury update

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Tottenham, featuring team news and expert analysis from Sam Tabuteau

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Roberto De Zerbi has admitted he has yet to decide whether to restore Pedro Porro to the starting lineup ahead of Tottenham’s trip to Leeds.

The Spaniard was named on the bench as Spurs made it three wins in a row with a 2-0 victory at home to Crystal Palace last weekend.

De Zerbi said after the game that the decision not to start the right-back, who recently returned to fitness after a month out with a hamstring injury, came from the player himself, who told his manager that he did not feel comfortable playing from the off.

Porro had initially injured his hamstring after insisting he was ready to start Spurs’ 3-2 defeat to Aston Villa in September before coming off injured after just 19 minutes.

De Zerbi was prepared to listen to his vice-captain ahead of the Palace game, even though he said post-match that he believed Porro was fit enough to start.

The Italian seemed confident that there were no issues with his full-back last weekend but he was reluctant to confirm whether he would be available to start at Elland Road.

“I have not decided yet about Pedro Porro, but it is not a tactical decision,” the head coach said.

“When Pedro Porro is fit, he plays because he is a top player, top guy, leader, clear leader, without problems.”

Porro has started just three of Spurs’ first nine league games this season, leaving Archie Gray to deputise in his absence.

The versatile Englishman has carved out an important role for himself in De Zerbi’s side and was promoted to the team’s five-man leadership group at the start of the season.

Gray leads the Premier League for underlapping runs and has made more sprints than any Spurs player this term, with De Zerbi describing him as a “very smart player”.

“Archie Gray is one of the best players in this moment, as a condition, as a confidence. He is playing very well,” De Zerbi continued.

“He can play everywhere because he can play right-back, full-back, midfielder. Is important overlapping, is important when he attack the space as a midfielder, starting from midfield. Important in the second ball, defensive phase, very smart player.”

Three years on, and Gray has developed into a jack of all trades, a leader on the pitch and someone De Zerbi believes can be instrumental to Spurs and England’s future.

Mikel Arteta laughs off Arsenal criticism amid Declan Rice injury concern

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Mikel Arteta has dismissed the suggestion that Arsenal are not managing Declan Rice's minutes carefully enough.

After playing 69 matches for club and country last season, the midfielder has again hit the ground running at the start of this campaign.

The midfielder has started all nine of the Gunners’ Premier League matches and has played the full 90 minutes in four of the last five.

Rice also started against Slavia Prague in midweek, while the likes of Martin Odegaard and Riccardo Calafiori dropped out of the starting lineup.

He played the full match despite looking extremely tired in the second half and will now join up with England, having missed the last camp due to load management.

However, Arteta issued a firm response when it was put to him that there could be concerns over Rice's workload.

He said: "Concerns? The only player, the only player in this squad that for two games in the Carabao Cup has been left out of the squad. At home with two days off. That's my concern.

"It's not true. We are really looking after him more than any other season. The way he's training and all the load. That's it.

"Here are the demands of this football club and you have a player like him. Not only him because there are many others that have even less time. That's it.

"That has to be the mindset. If you start to put other things in your brain, damage. Erase them, get them in the bin and just focus on what you have to do."

Arteta was just as forceful in insisting his side can have no excuses at the end of an unusual run of fixtures.

Of the 15 matches Arsenal have played this season, only five have been at home. In the past week, they have travelled to Fleetwood, Liverpool and Prague.

The Gunners make a welcome return to home comforts on Saturday to face Hull and Arteta reflected on what his side have had to deal with so far this season.

"It’s all a pleasure. I heard them tell me: ‘you guys fly with private jets and good buses and good food’. It’s beautiful. We have done it. It was what we had ahead of us, it was another challenge.

"Because normally you don’t play four away games in a row or three away games in a row. And you don’t play Sunday, Wednesday, Saturday - very strange.

"We have done it a few times and we have to do it tomorrow again. We are buzzing."

David Moyes Named the 4 Greatest Players He’s Ever Managed - Chose 2 Everton Icons

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

David Moyes has enjoyed a highly respectable managerial career over the years. Although his trophy cabinet might not be the most impressive – with West Ham United's Europa Conference League triumph in 2023 representing his biggest achievement – his remarkable longevity in the game deserves plenty of recognition.

Having managed in the Premier League since his appointment at Everton in 2002, he has established himself as one of the most successful managers in the competition's history, collecting more than 200 victories in the English top flight. After spells with Manchester United, Sunderland and West Ham in England, as well as a short period at Real Sociedad, he eventually returned to Everton in 2025.

Throughout his time managing several high-profile clubs, Moyes has worked with an abundance of outstanding footballing talent, including some of the greatest Premier League players of all time. Asked to identify the finest players he had managed by BBC Sport in 2024, the Scotsman named four individuals.

Wayne Rooney was a hugely gifted youngster who first came under Moyes' guidance at Everton before making his high-profile switch to Manchester United. The pair experienced some difficulties along the way, with the manager even taking the forward to court over a libel claim following criticism. Nevertheless, their relationship eventually improved sufficiently for them to reunite at Old Trafford.

Reflecting on Rooney's extraordinary ability, Moyes explained:

“Wayne Rooney: I think when I look back over the years, to have someone at such an early age be so talented, you know, known worldwide throughout his career, breaking Manchester United goalscoring records, breaking the England record for goalscoring… I think Wayne would have to be the standout player.”

When Moyes arrived at Manchester United, he inherited an exceptional defensive partnership featuring Nemanja Vidic and Rio Ferdinand. The squad also contained promising English centre-backs Phil Jones and Chris Smalling, but the former Everton boss clearly believed the Serbian defender, one of the greatest defenders in Premier League history, was in a class of his own, explaining:

"Vidic, I thought, was probably the best centre-half I'd worked with, I think I'd worked with a few at the time..."

From tough to downright terrifying, the hardest players of the Premier League era have been ranked.

Turning to a more recent example, Moyes' decision to highlight Declan Rice is hardly unexpected. The midfielder played a pivotal role in West Ham's Europa Conference League success in 2023 before completing a lucrative £100m transfer to Arsenal. Moyes clearly admired the England international's development and had plenty of positive things to say about him:

"At West Ham, for example, I had Declan Rice, who was another really young player, but one who was certainly on the rise and went on to fulfil his potential as a young player. I see him probably becoming an England captain in the near future.

Arguably the most unexpected inclusion among the four, Moyes appeared particularly pleased with the progress and achievements of Everton favourite Leon Osman. The former manager took great satisfaction from helping the midfielder earn international recognition, especially considering it came during the latter stages of his playing career:

"I think with people like Leon Osman, who was really really good player for us and went on to get an England cap when he was over 30, and sometimes those things give managers bigger thrills than some of the other boys."
