Mikel Arteta issues positive Bukayo Saka injury update amid new Arsenal return timeline

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Your weekly briefing on Arsenal, featuring team news and expert analysis from Matt Verri

I would like to be emailed about offers, event and updates from Evening Standard. Read our privacy notice.

Mikel Arteta is confident Bukayo Saka will make a successful recovery from his quad injury, with the Arsenal winger showing “some very good symptoms”.

Saka has missed the four matches the Gunners have played since he limped off in the opening ten minutes against Bayern Munich last month.

The initial expectation at the club was that the winger would be out for up to eight weeks, though scans were difficult due to the amount of fluid that had built up around the affected muscle.

The champions face Hull on Saturday in their final match before the international break and they will then return in a fortnight to start a heavyweight set of fixtures.

Between the end of November and Christmas, Arsenal face Borussia Dortmund, both Manchester clubs, Real Madrid, testing trips to Newcastle and Brentford, and a north London derby away from home.

Saka appears likely to miss many of those. A clearer timeline for his return will emerge after the international break, when Saka is expected to return to individual training on the grass.

When it was put to him that Saka could be out for at least another month, Arteta agreed but offered a positive update on how the early stages of the recovery have gone.

"I think so but I don't know,” Arteta said.

”The way it happened, it wasn't a really aggressive action. So that gives you some hope as well that the tissue will repair in a more efficient and faster way as well and he has some very good symptoms right now. So we have to wait and see."

Saka had made a bright start in that match against Bayern before picking up the injury in fairly innocuous fashion.

He burst away from Alphonso Davies but then collapsed to the ground as he played a pass out wide under no pressure.

Asked if Arsenal had got to the bottom of how and why Saka suffered the injury, Arteta replied: "Yeah, there are a few things that we believe can contribute.

“I prefer not to mention that here. But in reality as well to put a finger on something, maybe after you are totally wrong because there was something else. So it's very difficult.

“We have obviously thought and think and openly discussed a lot of things even with him to understand what could possibly have gone wrong. But the reality probably is that unfortunately we don't know.

Being ‘Good at Social Media’ Isn’t Enough to Run Your Business Accounts. Here’s What Is.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Invest in training your team, and your social media stops being a question mark and starts becoming one of your strongest business assets.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Small businesses do this every day. They hand off the Instagram social media account to a niece because she’s “always on her phone,” or have the youngest person in the office to run the social accounts by default. It’s understandable, and honestly, it’s a start! But managing social media for a business is a whole different ballgame. It takes real strategy, platform know-how, consistent execution and a clear understanding of what actually drives growth.

Here’s what I’ve learned working with clients across all kinds of industries: enthusiasm without training only gets you so far. Organic social media is one of the most powerful tools your business has, and it only delivers when the person running it knows what exactly they are doing. Here are seven reasons why training your social media team is one of the smartest moves you can make.

Paid ads are great, but organic social media is where your brand actually lives. It is how people discover you, decide if they trust you and choose to spend their money with you. Done well, it builds community, drives traffic and keeps you top of mind without a single dollar in ad spend. When I work with clients at Fix Your Search, one of the first things I do is help their team understand just how powerful this tool is, because most people underestimate it until they see a solid organic strategy in action.

That kind of impact does not happen by accident. It requires strategy, consistency and someone who understands how each platform works. Training is what gets you there.

This is the big one. Scrolling TikTok every night does not make someone qualified to manage your brand’s presence. Personal use and professional strategy are completely different skill sets. When I start training a client’s team, we talk about the difference between posting for fun and posting with purpose. Once that clicks, everything else starts to fall into place.

Here is the tricky part a lot of business owners do not talk about. If social media strategy is not in your forte, how do you teach it to someone else? You cannot spot mistakes you cannot see. When a client brings me in, the business owner does not have to be the expert. I take their team members from the very beginning, walking them through what good social media management actually looks like, step by step, so there is no guesswork on anyone’s end.

What works on Instagram is not what works on LinkedIn. What thrives on TikTok might flop on Facebook. Each platform has its own audience behavior, content format and algorithm logic, and not knowing the difference is one of the fastest ways to waste your team’s time and energy.

Platform-specific training is a core part of how I work with my clients. We always start with the fundamentals: understanding each platform’s purpose, writing captions that earn engagement, building a content calendar that makes sense and reading analytics without the eyes glazing over. That foundation is everything.

The algorithm loves relevance. Trending formats, sounds and topics get shown to more people, plain and simple. But jumping on a trend blindly without understanding how to make it work for your brand can hurt more than help. That is why trend awareness is a dedicated part of the training I do with my clients. When a team knows how to work with trends, reach can grow fast and it is always exciting to watch it happen.

Training is not one single afternoon session. Social media platforms change constantly, and a strategy that worked six months ago might be hurting your reach today. When I train a client’s team, I stay involved over time. We review what is working, adjust to algorithm updates and keep building on what the team learns. Everything I do is tailored to the specific business, because what makes sense for a local bakery looks completely different from what makes sense for a nightclub or an upscale restaurant.

Your social media accounts are often the very first impression a potential customer has of your business. That’s why, when I train a client’s team, I always tie the work back to this bigger picture. We’re not just filling a content calendar. We are crafting a presence that earns trust, attracts the right people and reflects the business at its best.

When proper training is in place, the results speak for themselves. Engagement climbs, reach grows and the team member who once second-guessed every post starts owning their role with real confidence. Watching that shift happen is honestly the most rewarding part of the work I do.

If your social media is not delivering the results you hoped for, the answer is almost never to scrap everything and start fresh. More often than not, the missing piece is proper training. Organic social media is a long game, and every business that plays it well has one thing in common: a team that genuinely knows what they are doing, why they are doing it and how to keep getting better. That kind of expertise does not appear overnight, but it is absolutely buildable. Invest in training your team the right way, and your social media stops being a question mark and starts becoming one of your strongest business assets.

Small businesses do this every day. They hand off the Instagram social media account to a niece because she’s “always on her phone,” or have the youngest person in the office to run the social accounts by default. It’s understandable, and honestly, it’s a start! But managing social media for a business is a whole different ballgame. It takes real strategy, platform know-how, consistent execution and a clear understanding of what actually drives growth.

Organic social media is one of the most powerful tools your business has, and it only delivers when the person running it knows what exactly they are doing. Here are seven reasons why training your social media team is one of the smartest moves you can make.

Done well, it builds community, drives traffic and keeps you top of mind without a single dollar in ad spend. When I work with clients at Fix Your Search, one of the first things I do is help their team understand just how powerful this tool is, because most people underestimate it until they see a solid organic strategy in action.

Derided Thierno Barry is no longer a source of frustration as star fires Everton to victory over Coventry on another big night for David Moyes amid Jack Grealish drama

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Maybe Everton should request to play under the lights every week. It certainly seems to push David Moyes' team to new heights.

Following hot on the heels of consecutive night victories over Newcastle United, Friday's 3-1 win against Coventry City was a reminder of why, under Moyes, there is renewed sense of optimism at Everton.

Up to fifth, above Liverpool, and in a Carabao Cup quarter-final at Fleetwood, it could prove to be a special season.

This always felt like a big task for Frank Lampard, back at Everton for the first time since his sacking in 2023.

Popular as the ex-manager may be, there's no room for sentiment, certainly not from Moyes on a night he took charge of his 592nd game, drawing level with Harry Catterick for managing the most games in the club's history.

He'll set it outright after the international break against Brentford and Keith Andrews, who was 21 when Moyes first took up the reins at Everton.

Thierno Barry (right) now has 10 goals this season after being widely derided in his first season on Merseyside

Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall had been left out of the England squad in the morning, but refused to let it spoil his night

His side started impressively, fashioning three shots in the opening 10 minutes, before losing all momentum and allowing Coventry into proceedings.

It took going 1-0 down, to a soft Yann Gboho goal, for the hosts to come alive. Thierno Barry, so often the source of frustration last season, was magnificent and all but won the game with a five-minute brace in the second half.

The striker didn't score his first goal until December last season. This campaign, he's reached 10 before the second international break. Moyes joked post-match that Erling Haaland should watch his back in the Golden Boot race is starting to feel less absurd. Barry now sits joint second in the charts.

Both goals against Coventry came via back post-duels. The first he confidently headed past Carl Rushworth, before bundling in a second five minutes later.

The disgruntled home fans booed the team off at half-time, yet were jubilantly cheering and signing after Barry's brace. The Frenchman danced on the pitch at full-time and was serenaded by the South Stand.

'I don't see loads of young centre-forwards scoring loads of goals in the Premier League,' said Moyes.

'He's come in and has settled in a bit more. His language and communication is better and obviously he's worked to get himself into the positions to score.'

Barry wasn't the only Everton goalscorer, of course. Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall was not one of the three Everton players named in Thomas Tuchel's England squad on Friday - only Arsenal had more representatives.

EVERTON (4-2-3-1): Pickford 5; Rohl 5 (O'Brien 73min, 6), Tarkowski 5, Branthwaite 6 (Keane 89), Mykolenko 6; Armstrong 6 (Hackney 73, 6), Garner 6; Dibling 5 (Johnson 73, 5), DEWSBURY-HALL 8, George 5 (Alcaraz 90); Barry 8.

COVENTRY CITY (4-2-3-1): Rushworth 4; van Ewijk 5, Mfuni 6, Pinnock 6, Dasilva 6; Yirenkyi 6, Torp 6 (Grimes 86); Sakamoto 5 (Tchaouna 86), Rudoni 5 (Hamer 86), Gboho 6 (Thomas-Asante 73, 5); Awoniyi 5 (Cherif 87).

But the midfielder sent a pointed message to the Three Lions' boss with a superb showing that was capped with a late powerful goal to end the contest.

'Yeah, sad, but really proud of myself and how I have reacted. All I can keep doing is putting in good performances and hopefully one day I get the nod, because I'm never going to give up. I've got the mentality. I'll keep pushing and pushing and see what happens.'

'He's desperate to play for England,' added Moyes. 'You never know if England get a couple of injuries, or anything changes.'

What ended as a comfortable win could have been a different outcome had Jame Tarkowski been punished for a hair pull on Caleb Yirenkyi, or if Vitalii Mykolenko's tussle with Taiwo Awoniyi in the box had been deemed a foul.

Both were checked and cleared by VAR, as was a handball claim in the build-up to Barry's second goal.

'Tarky pulls on Caleb's hair, they [VAR] are saying it's not enough of a pull,' said an aggrieved Lampard afterwards. 'That's a hard area to be in. What is hair pull and what's not?

'I think Taiwo, on the edge of the box was a penalty. I feel there's a penalty by the time Myko's knee is in the back of Taiwo's knee on the line of the box.

'Then the Barry one is handball. He flicks it in [with the hand] with the final touch. Nothing went for us tonight.'

Everton 3-1 Coventry City: The forward’s form as the club’s only recognised striker is proving a major boost. Only Erling Haaland has scored more than him in the League

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Everton climbed into the top five after coming from behind to claim a 3-1 win over Coventry, powered by more goals from Thierno Barry.

The 24-year-old’s double flipped the contest against former Toffees manager Frank Lampard’s side. Only Manchester City forward Erling Haaland, with nine, has registered more league goals this season.

Barry’s unpolished moments can create frustration, demonstrated in the first half when he wasted a clear shooting opening by failing to sort his feet out, but he proved effective inside the six-yard box after the restart.

Having fallen behind to Yann Gboho’s first goal for the Sky Blues on his second league appearance, the Frenchman scored a header to equalise before adding a second by scrambling the ball in from barely two yards.

Lampard will feel his team should have received at least one penalty after James Tarkowski appeared to pull Caleb Yirenkyi’s hair while the game was goalless in the first half, though Coventry’s defensive work left much to be desired.

It is the first time Barry has scored in three straight matches for Everton, and only the second time in his career.

He has now scored six in his last seven games across all competitions and 10 for the season, two more than he recorded during his inaugural campaign.

His display aligned with pre-match remarks from boss David Moyes, who equalled Harry Catterick’s record of 592 matches in charge, having stated that "Erling Haaland should be worried".

And while realistically the Norway international will not be losing any sleep at the thought, Barry’s form as the club’s only recognised striker is proving a major boost.

With Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall chipping in with goals from midfield, scoring the third nine minutes from time, to add to his all-round creativity Moyes is squeezing everything out of his limited resources.

A third successive win also took the focus off the circus surrounding Jack Grealish.

He has been absent for the last four matches after being disciplined by the club for missing training, but he returned to Finch Farm earlier in the day to begin a tailored programme which is likely to see him return to the first-team fold after the international break.

A nondescript first half which began with Everton in the ascendency finished with the visitors in command and was notable mainly for Tarkowski’s tangle with Yirenkyi as they challenged for a cross.

His grasp of the midfielder’s hair was so slight it appeared to go unnoticed by everyone except the player and no action was taken by VAR.

Coventry’s second penalty appeal was checked by the officials but Vitalii Mykolenko’s challenge on Taiwo Awoniyi was ruled to have been made fractionally outside the box.

But when the ball bounced around the area after the visitors were able to play their way from right to left Gboho, surrounded by three Everton players, poked through a shot which appeared to take Pickford by surprise as it crept over the line.

The lead lasted four minutes as Barry barely had to get off the ground to head home Mykolenko’s cross after Jay Dasilva crumpled to the floor.

Within five minutes they were ahead as he again outjumped Dasilva at the far post to bundle home off his midriff with a panicked goalkeeper Carl Rushworth unable to keep it out.

Dewsbury-Hall made the result safe with a fine angled finish to displace Merseyside neighbours Liverpool in fifth place.

Worker or queen? A genetic advantage may shape a fire ant’s future

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A study of fire ants suggests a “selfish” supergene influences body size, potentially giving some ants a better chance of becoming queens.

Most red imported fire ants become workers. A few become queens, the largest ants in the colony and the ones that lay eggs.

How large an ant grows during development helps determine whether it becomes a worker or a queen.

A new study suggests that genes also influence this process.

In colonies with several queens, ants carrying a particular version of a large group of genes were more common among the biggest workers and future queens.

Haolin “Horace” Zeng and Brendan Hunt, insect researchers at the University of Georgia, studied this pattern in lab colonies.

Their work offers a clue to why these genes keep spreading, even though they can have drawbacks for the ants carrying them.

Some fire ant colonies have just one queen laying eggs, while others have many. A group of more than 500 genes helps determine which kind of colony develops.

These genes sit close together on a chromosome, a structure that carries DNA. They pass from parent to offspring together as a package called a supergene.

“We call them supergenes because the genes are tightly linked and inherited together, almost like one big gene,” explained Hunt, an associate professor in UGA’s entomology department.

The package comes in two versions. Scientists call the older version B and the newer one b, which appeared about one million years ago.

Ants in single-queen colonies have two copies of B, written as BB.

In invasive populations with several queens per colony, every egg-laying queen has one copy of each version, written as Bb.

Queens with the Bb combination weigh less and have more trouble starting colonies alone than BB queens. Yet the b version continues to spread.

Scientists describe it as a “selfish genetic element” because it improves its own chances of reaching the next generation, even when it does not help the ant carrying it.

Becoming a queen gives a female ant the opportunity to pass her genes to the next generation.

“If you want to pass down a gene, you’d better become a queen. You don’t want to become a worker,” Zeng said.

Young ants grow as larvae before becoming pupae, the stage when they change into adults. A natural chemical called juvenile hormone can delay the move out of the larval stage, giving them more time to eat and grow.

The researchers wanted to find out whether ants with the b version responded differently to this hormone.

In spring 2024, they collected colonies with one queen and colonies with several queens from Clarke and Oconee counties in Georgia.

They used these ants to set up small lab colonies without queens.

Each held about 2,500 workers, and all colonies received the same total weight of young ants at different developmental stages.

Some colonies received one meal containing methoprene, an artificial chemical that acts like juvenile hormone and is also used as an insecticide.

The others received a meal without it, and workers passed the food to the larvae.

Between 15 and 24 days after that meal, the team collected and weighed worker pupae. The middle weight, or median, was about three times higher in treated colonies than in untreated colonies.

The researchers then checked the genes of 1,049 worker pupae and 195 queen pupae from colonies with several queens. They compared the ants’ genes with their weight.

Ants carrying the b version became more common as body weight increased.

Here, ‘carrying the supergene’ refers specifically to the b version.

“Among the smallest workers, about half carried the supergene,” Hunt said. “That proportion increased to roughly 65% among the largest workers and 75% to 83% among queens.”

On average, BB pupae weighed about 19% less than Bb pupae. But the heaviest ants in each group weighed about the same, so the b version did not raise the upper limit on size.

Instead, ants carrying b reached the larger sizes more often.

The hormone treatment increased size by about the same amount in both groups, suggesting that a major difference in their response to this hormone did not explain the pattern.

The share of Bb ants also rose with weight at a similar rate among workers and queens.

That suggests the gene package affects body size, which then helps shape whether an ant becomes a queen, rather than having a separate effect on queen development.

“To me, it highlights that, through a developmental mechanism involving body size, a genetic element can alter caste outcomes and basically cheat its way into making a queen,” Hunt said.

The team next looked for genes that might explain the size difference. Scientists do not yet have a full record of which genes become more active as fire ant larvae grow into workers or queens.

The researchers therefore used existing data from pharaoh ants, which belong to the same ant subfamily.

Earlier work on adult fire ants had identified 191 genes that were more active in Bb ants than in BB ants.

Of those, 34 were also more active in pharaoh ant larvae developing into queens. That overlap was larger than the researchers would expect by chance.

One gene, called gas8, appeared in every comparison and sits within the fire ant supergene.

DNA near the start of gas8 is also more loosely packed in Bb fire ants, which fits with the gene being more active.

The team still does not know how the gene package changes growth.

Perhaps large BB larvae die more often before becoming pupae, or perhaps some Bb larvae spend longer eating and growing before moving to the next stage.

A computer simulation showed that extra growing time could produce the pattern seen in the study. Giving Bb larvae a better chance of continuing to a larger size could also explain it.

Workers might treat larvae carrying b more favorably, but that alone seems unlikely to explain the findings.

An earlier experiment found extra Bb queen pupae even when only BB workers raised them.

Researchers have not yet tested what gas8 does as ants develop. Experiments with fire ant larvae will be needed to find out.

Comparisons with other animals that carry supergenes could answer a wider question: do these gene packages often spread by changing how animals grow, or how long they spend growing?

The study is published in the journal Proceedings of the Royal Society B.

What Runners Need to Know About Rhabdo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After a tragic death in the running community, doctors want runners to know the risk factors for the condition.

Shortly after the New York City Marathon, tragic news broke: A 24-year-old runner, Rachel Apa, collapsed on the course and died in the hospital after doctors worked for six hours to save her life, according to the New York Times. Apa’s father told NYT the cause of death was rhabdomyolysis (a.k.a. rhabdo).

The news is shocking and saddening—as well as unsettling. Questions are popping up from runners on social media as to how something like this could happen to a young woman who was reportedly a former Division I field hockey player at Bucknell University and had spent months training for the marathon.

To help answer some of those questions, we talked to doctors to explain what runners need to know about rhabdo, and how to stay safe.

First, know that rhabdo is relatively rare and dying from it is even rarer. Approximately 26,000 cases of rhabdomyolysis are reported annually in the United States, and the mortality rate starts at 10 percent but may go up to 50 percent in severe cases.

In short, rhabdo is a breakdown of muscle that in and of itself is not life-threatening. In fact, Derek M. Fine, MD, a nephrologist at Johns Hopkins Medicine, says that if you were to test creatine kinase (a.k.a. CK, a blood marker of muscle breakdown) in marathon runners after the race, “most” of them would show elevated levels of the enzyme.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says that multiple CK tests are the only way to accurately identify rhabdo, though. Normal CK is between 20 and 200 U/L, but rhabdo is diagnosed when it reaches five times the upper limit.

“There’s always a little bit of muscle breakdown and the reason is because, at least in [a] marathon, you’re using the muscles so much that at some point they can run out of an energy supply,” Fine says, adding that the mitochondria can't keep up and make enough of what’s called ATP (adenosine triphosphate), which is your primary source of energy.

Rhabdo becomes dangerous when it becomes more severe. As muscle tissue breaks down, the body can release harmful substances into the bloodstream, including myoglobin, a protein that can damage the kidneys, and excessive amounts of potassium.

As for myoglobin, the protein can get “stuck” in the kidney and cause kidney failure or injury at various levels, Fine says. “It can be quite severe, but that usually takes a little bit of time to manifest,” he explains.

Fine says that high levels of potassium are probably a bigger risk than myoglobin. “When the muscle cells break up, you get a very large release of potassium into the bloodstream and that potassium then can cause cardiac arrhythmias,” he says. This can cause kidney failure (your kidneys are the organ tasked with getting rid of extra potassium) or potentially sudden cardiac arrest.

“Under normal circumstances, if it’s happening slowly and you’ve got decent kidneys, you pee the potassium out and it never gets that high in the blood,” he says. “If there's severe rhabdo and you have a huge amount of muscles breaking down, you can release a ton of potassium and your body can't get rid of it fast enough.”

In addition to the rapid release of these substances, rhabdo can sometimes lead to other issues that resemble sepsis or even multisystem organ failure, Fine says.

Still, rhabdo is not a common underlying cause of cardiac arrest, says Lili Barouch, MD, director of sports cardiology at Johns Hopkins Medicine and an endurance athlete herself. However, it can lead to abnormal heart rhythms due to severe electrolyte problems, primarily high potassium. And as Fine also explains, kidney failure (as well as other organ failure) can result in cardiac problems, she says.

It’s not just running that can increase risk of rhabdo—any heavy exercise can do damage to muscles, Barouch says, particularly exertional rhabdomyolysis. In fact, Fine says that there have been incidents of rhabdo reported in CrossFit athletes for this reason. Whether it’s a marathon or weight lifting, it’s in high-effort situations that the muscle breakdown can accelerate rapidly and lead to problems.

As for running, while training does induce microscopic damage to muscles that later repairs in a stronger way—a positive outcome—many people push harder in a marathon. “For a race, you’re usually going at a much higher level of effort and you’re really pushing yourself to your limit,” Barouch says.

This can be true for athletes of any level, but people who aren’t as highly trained who push really hard on race day may be at higher risk, Fine says. “The most common reason [for getting rhabdo] is you’ve got someone who overdoes it and who wasn’t trained enough,” he says. (This was not necessarily the case with Apa, just a potential risk factor for runners in general.)

Another risk factor is dehydration, Barouch says. “If someone’s dehydrated, the amount of those muscle breakdown products [your body produces] that can become toxic to the kidneys starts to really go up. You're unable to clear the toxins out of your body fast enough and that can cause more damage,” she says.

On the flip side, good hydration can help flush the myoglobin from the kidneys, per Fine. But, he points out that there’s a delicate balance between being over- and underhydrated: “While you’re running a marathon, your kidneys are getting messages from your brain to hold on to water, so if you keep putting water in there, the water dilutes out the sodium in the blood and you can get hyponatremia,” he says. The key: Drink to thirst and take electrolytes during a long or hot race, Fine says.

Carbohydrates could help as well. “Part of rhabdo could be if you’re not giving enough carbohydrates and energy [to your body], you can't make ATP [the energy currency you need to perform] and that’s where the muscle could break down,” Fine says.

Another factor for marathon runners is environmental conditions. “If it’s a particularly hot day, for example, that seems to be a big risk factor,” Fine says. In fact, there was an unusually large incidence (24 cases) of exertional rhabdomyolysis at the 2022 NYC Marathon, per a study in Nephron Clinical Practice, likely associated with the “unseasonably high heat and humidity” on race day, per the authors.

Younger runners may actually be at higher risk of rhabdo, likely because they’re more inclined to take risks and push harder than the older population, Fine says, potentially ignoring signs of rhabdo.

There’s also a genetic component to rhabdo, Fine says. So, if you have had rhabdo before, or have a family member who has had rhabdo, it could be worth looking into your own risk more closely.

It’s not super common, but genetic tests look at certain markers that might suggest someone is more susceptible to rhabdo. “It’s not like you're going to say, ‘Okay, I’m going to run a marathon, I’m going to go do prescreening genetic testing,’” Fine says. “But in someone who may have had this before, even if they said, ‘Well, I did CrossFit and I got rhabdo and I'm now going to try to run a marathon,’ that sort of person may be worth checking.”

Finally, certain medications (including statins and certain antidepressants) and underlying conditions (like McArdle disease and Duchenne muscular dystrophy) might predispose someone to rhabdo, Barouch says.

The experts agree that signs of rhabdo can be hard to identify because aches are pretty much unavoidable when running a marathon. Still, severe pain in the working muscles (e.g., the legs) is a red flag, Fine says, as are muscle weakness and swelling.

“Of course during a marathon, people tend to have muscle pain, but if it’s much more severe than usual or much more severe than expected,” that’s what to watch for, per Barouch. Severe muscle soreness after the race is also something to keep an eye on, she adds.

Another thing to look out for is urine color. “What one might see early on is very sort of dark orange, rust-colored urine, and that’s the myoglobin coming out,” Fine says. “If someone’s got muscle pain and their urine’s orange, they probably want to go and get checked out and maybe get hydrated and get a CK level checked in an emergency room.”

Per the CDC, the earlier you get treated, the more likely you’ll recover without long-lasting or permanent health effects, so don’t wait if you suspect you have rhabdo.

At the end of the day, it comes down to listening to your body. “As endurance athletes especially, we tend to push ourselves really hard for a race because we have personal goals, time goals, things like that, but you still have to listen to your body,” Barouch says.

If something feels off, don’t ignore it. “Marathons are not comfortable to run for anybody, but if your muscles seem extremely painful or if you start to have other symptoms, if you’re having breathing problems, chest problems, things like that, that seem out of the ordinary, [do] not ignore those warning signs either for rhabdo or another medical condition that might be building up,” Barouch says.

Manchester United handed major transfer boost as key target 'up for sale'

Manchester United have been bolstered in their pursuit of a left-back.

The Red Devils have been hamstrung in that position this term, with natural options Luke Shaw and Patrick Dorgu both struck down with injuries that have kept them out for long spells.

As such, it has forced under-fire manager Michael Carrick to experiment with makeshift solutions in the interim, with right-backs Diogo Dalot and Noussair Mazraoui both depuitising there, while central defender Ayden Heaven has also been shifted out wide to cover with limited success.

As such, it makes the role a key position that needs strengthening at Old Trafford, with many United fans believing that a left-back should have been signed in the summer.

Newcastle United’s Lewis Hall was thought to be the priority target, but Barcelona’s Alejandro Balde was also a reported option.

Should United reignite their interest in the Spaniard, they could well find a deal with their Catalan rivals relatively easy to come by.

That is because The Athletic reports that Hansi Flick’s side want to sell Balde, a stance they have held since the summer transfer window.

They could not sanction a sale of the defender, who was keen to try and play his way back into his manager’s plans despite being told that he was surplus to requirements.

Barcelona’s cash-strapped situtation - as well as their need to raise funds to sign a new left-sided centre-back - will also make them more desperate to sell Balde.

Despite the 23-year-old’s willingness to stay at his boyhood club, he has been limited to just 124 minutes this term.

With Shaw’s contract up at the end of the season and Dorgu not a true left-back under current boss Carrick, a deal for Balde could well prove an enticing, cut-price option for the Red Devils.

Why the Henley Is Fall’s Most Unexpected Trend. Here’s Why You Should Care

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Consider this your sign to rethink the most underrated shirt.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

THEY’VE BEEN CROPPING up everywhere. All over my social media feed, featured across men’s runway collections at Fashion Week, seen on Hollywood heartthrobs like Michael B. Jordan, and so forth. If you haven’t figured it out by now, I’m talking about the henley. This once-overlooked wardrobe staple has found new popularity, showing no signs of slowing down as it becomes one of fall’s most surprising menswear trends. And quite frankly, I’m here for it.

The henley hasn’t traditionally been the sexiest shirt in a guy’s wardrobe. For years, it’s been the tee you’d throw on for a lazy Sunday or wear underneath a flannel. But somewhere along the way, the fashion crowd decided it deserved another shot. Its roots go all the way back to 19th-century England, where it started as an undergarment before becoming a go-to for rowers in Henley-on-Thames, hence the name. Who would’ve thought a shirt with such humble beginnings would eventually become a fashion obsession on my Instagram timeline?

Cos Slim Ribbed Cotton Long-Sleeved Henley T-Shirt

Todd Snyder Lightweight Mini Waffle Henley

Cherry Los Angeles Waffle-Knit Cotton-Blend Henley T-Shirt

California Arts Murray Slim Long Sleeve Henley

Here’s what I love about the henley: It does more heavy lifting than your average T-shirt without trying to be anything fancy. You get the simplicity of a crewneck, the button placket of a polo, and just enough texture to make an otherwise basic outfit feel put-together. And while the classic cotton version is still very much in rotation, ribbed, waffle-knit, and thermal styles are giving guys even more ways to wear it. If you need further proof that the henley is having a moment, just take a peek at the runways. For Fall/Winter 2026, Paul Smith sent a model down the runway in a white henley tucked into wide-leg black trousers, while Isabel Marant took a more layered approach, pairing a light blue henley with a navy corduroy jacket.

I’m a firm believer that proportions can make or break an outfit, and the henley is no exception. My favorite way to wear one is with baggy jeans or relaxed trousers. The contrast between a fitted top and roomier bottoms creates a flattering silhouette. And if you’ve spent the past few months putting in work at the gym, a henley is one of the easiest ways to show off those gains without looking like you’re showing off. The closer fit naturally accentuates your chest, shoulders, and biceps, especially when you leave the top button or two undone. Just remember, there’s a fine line between fitted and too tight.

Whether you’re dressing one up with trousers and loafers or keeping things casual with loose jeans and boots, the henley proves that sometimes the most unexpected trends are the pieces we’ve had in our closets all along. And it’s about time this one received its flowers.

The AI posting war is heating up: 'Please, leave me behind,' says artist who got into it with Epic CEO Tim Sweeney on X

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join the club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Your weekly update on everything you could ever want to know about the games you already love, games we know you're going to love in the near future, and tales from the communities that surround them.

Get sneak previews, exclusive competitions and details of special events each month!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Two factions of gamers and game developers are at an impasse.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Kenney, an artist known for giving away thousands of free game assets, did not expect to wind up directly arguing with specific billionaires after telling off billionaires in general in a recent X post. But a few hours after he encouraged other artists not to give in to pressure to use generative AI, Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney was in his replies.

"This is bullshit, Kenney," Sweeney told the artist, concluding his rebuttal with a cute double entendre: "I've upped my expectations of AI. Up yours!"

Richest-person-in-the-world Elon Musk also chimed in to back up Sweeney. Hundreds of other comments on the exchange inevitably followed, spanning the full spectrum of AI opinions.

Kenney's contentious post was: "Don't let billionaires convince you slop is the future. They're used to managing & directing, not making things themselves. Of course they can't see the difference. They don't understand your love for the craft. Keep making personal, beautiful games. You've got my support 💖"

The artist had not had Sweeney in mind when he wrote the post; it was a response to something Musk had said. Kenney expected it to be read by other artists who may feel pressured to use generative AI despite objecting to it.

"I think AI has some benefit but there's just too many problems with it that I find it immoral to use in its current state," Kenney told PC Gamer in a chat after that exchange. "Whether it's the environmental issues, data centers, theft during training, or the negative impact it has on pretty much every industry."

Sweeney is not known to keep his opinions to himself, but the Epic CEO has been particularly active in his defense of generative AI on X recently.

As a provider of technology to the whole industry, I see it as Epic's job and my job to make the positive case for it.

While some companies use AI but stay quiet about it—Baldur's Gate 3 developer Larian received substantial pushback for discussing limited AI use last year, as an example of how the conversation can go—Epic has made its support for AI tools unambiguous. The company is going "all-in" on AI-assisted game development with its next Unreal Engine release, proudly calls the new version of its Epic Games Store launcher "vibe coded," and recently announced an AI-focused game jam.

Meanwhile, Sweeney has joined likeminded X users in battling what they see as online persecution from an anti-AI "hate mob."

"Because speaking in favor of AI draws such harsh criticism, there is great reluctance among most professional AI adopters in gaming to speak up," the Epic CEO told PC Gamer this week. "As a provider of technology to the whole industry, I see it as Epic's job and my job to make the positive case for it."

According to Sweeney, opposition to AI in games is not a popular view. He has described the anti-AI crowd as "a pessimistic minority running a venomous, vocal pressure campaign to force compliance and silence dissent," and claimed this week that most opposition to AI comes from "astroturfed anti-AI influence campaigns."

PC Gamer has not seen any evidence for that last claim, but it is the case that "AI slop" accusations on social media have not been matched by a massive rejection of AI from the broader gaming audience.

The generative AI use in Arc Raiders led to a 2/5 review in Eurogamer, but the extraction shooter nevertheless sold over 16 million copies. Several games on Steam's current top sellers list also disclose AI use, including Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4, Aion 4, MapleStory, and indie game Moon Sweepers, which just announced 600,000 sales.

A recent survey of Steam users by GameDiscoverCo found that the majority, about 69%, are either "neutral," "fine with," or "have absolutely no problem with" buying games that disclose the use of AI in their development, something Steam requires for certain applications of AI.

However, the survey found that the disclosures themselves are popular. According to its results, most Steam users check them before buying a game, and over 55% of respondents think Steam should have even stricter AI disclosure rules.

Sweeney is staunchly against Steam's disclosure requirement, calling it "abhorrent" and claiming that it forces developers to make a political statement that incites "cancel campaigns and public struggle sessions" against them.

Kenney's read on the situation is the opposite of Sweeney's: that a minority which includes billionaire tech executives is pushing hard to normalize generative AI tools and get everyone hooked on them despite resistance from a public that wants to see "soul and love put into games."

"If it were true and AI has had mass adoption, why even bother going after me or trying to convince me to start using it?" Kenney asks.

"I'd rather leave the industry someday when I'm old(...er) and have everyone remember me for the good stuff I did and the many people I've helped, rather than for lining my own pockets.

Some gamers do support the use of AI—or, as established, are at least willing to accept the use of generative AI tools—but even as OpenAI boasts over a billion monthly active users, it's easy to find people who share Kenney's total distaste of AI, and the conversation is not limited to mean X posts.

As of the most recent Game Developers Conference survey, over 50% of devs think AI is bad for the industry. Wariness of data centers crosses party lines in the US, as we just saw in the midterm elections. A large survey of PC Gamer readers recently came back strongly against generative AI in games.

One developer went so far as to cancel their Epic Games Store release in support of Kenney. "We just don't wanna be associated with such an embarrassing platform," said the studio, Dead Teapot.

Kenney is a particularly appropriate figure for the anti-AI game development crowd to rally around, embodying the communal, cooperative artmaking spirit that big AI is seen to threaten. For over a decade, Kenney has been releasing free assets for game developers. It started with leftover art from abandoned Flash game projects, and now the artist makes a living from donations, as well as sales of a $20 bundle that packages all 60,000 assets into one download.

"It isn't a lot, which isn't too surprising if you give nearly everything away for free, but it's enough to [get] by," says Kenney. "I'd rather leave the industry someday when I'm old(...er) and have everyone remember me for the good stuff I did and the many people I've helped, rather than for lining my own pockets."

Meanwhile, Sweeney argues that productivity-aids like generative AI are necessary to make game development economically viable today. He recently reposted an AI-generated cartoon depicting a dev's game launch being ruined by an angry anti-AI mob after they used AI to cut their costs, claiming: "This is exactly what's happening."

I'm worried that the part of the industry I love is going to disappear or be taken over by people who aren't in it for the love of the craft, but rather for the grift.

In the back-and-forth that followed Sweeney's rebuttal to Kenney's "don't let billionaires convince you slop is the future" post, the Epic CEO told Kenney that his reason for pushing back is that "there's risk that convincing devs to abstain from the new way leaves them behind."

"I'm concerned that development studios swept up in these anti-AI campaigns will make decisions that render themselves uncompetitive in the global market for games," Sweeney told PC Gamer this week. He predicts that studios will see "productivity gains of at least 2x to 4x" with AI tools, and says that "all developers need to be prepared for this."

For Kenney and likeminded developers and players, however, a game development scene that's all-in on generative AI would not be a more efficient continuation of the same scene they helped build, but a betrayal of it—something they fundamentally don't want to be a part of.

"I'm not worried about myself or my own studio," says Kenney. "I'm worried that the part of the industry I love is going to disappear or be taken over by people who aren't in it for the love of the craft, but rather for the grift."

And regarding the concern that developers who don't embrace AI will be left behind: "Please, leave me behind," says Kenney. "I'm pretty happy here."

Tyler grew up in Silicon Valley during the '80s and '90s, playing games like Zork and Arkanoid on early PCs. He was later captivated by Myst, SimCity, Civilization, Command & Conquer, all the shooters they call "boomer shooters" now, and PS1 classic Bushido Blade (that's right: he had Bleem!). Tyler joined PC Gamer in 2011, and today he's focused on the site's news coverage. His hobbies include amateur boxing and adding to his 1,200-plus hours in Rocket League.

Kawasaki Z1000, Yamaha FZ6 Quad, Subaru Sambar: The Dopest Cars I Found For Sale Online

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Happy whatever day it is, friends. It's been a ride this past week — things have been weird, and almost exclusively in good ways. You remember good things, right? Turns out, those can still happen! This whole time, while things have been weird and bad, they could have been weird and good instead. What a concept!

Today, in honor of that, we're looking at some fascinating listings from across Facebook Marketplace. Not just cool cars or good deals, but trying to find the specifically weird and interesting — things you wouldn't necessarily expect, but are happy to see. We've got suspiciously cheap bikes, kei trucks, even a completely banal and unremarkable commuter car from 2013. What's less expected around these parts than that? So come in, take a load off, and let's walk hand in hand through the new world we've wrought. Well, its car listings at least. Welcome, my friends, to this week's Dopest Cars.

Did you know Kawasaki naked literbikes cost under seven grand used? I had no idea, but this Z1000 for $6,999 feels like a genuinely great price. This isn't some clapped-out Hayabusa for cheap, it's a modern bike that seems to be in good condition, one that's actually enjoyable to use as a daily rider. Want to go a million miles per hour on the cheap, without having a bike that's a pain to lug through your commute? The Z1000 awaits.

I will say, for those eyeing this bike, that you should probably budget for some extra expenses. It looks like whatever tail tidy this Z has is one that combines the rear turn signals into the brake light, which is difficult for drivers to see — take it from someone who's put low-profile blinkers on a bike before, I felt safer with lights on stalks. If I were to buy this, I'd want to add some properly visible turn signals back to the rear end.

I've long been browsing the livable vehicle market, but my recent renewed seriousness and urgency in shopping for vans and campers has had an odd side effect: I've gotten extremely brown-beige-RV-pilled. I don't know how or why it happened, but suddenly I'm looking at these deeply '80s-looking campers and thinking about how much I want one. I don't think I could stand to live in a house that looked as dated as this Econoline camper, but put it on wheels and suddenly I'm sold.

I do wonder, in a camper this old, how much of a risk of mold there is. After all, these things live outside, and seals are known to fail. I worry about mold in New York apartments all the same, and least this would let me own a dwelling that I could keep around if the economy tanks and I get booted from my apartment. That's been on my mind recently, for no particular reason.

I'm not much of a drag girlie, in either automotive or nightlife terms, but this RX-8 dragster deserves special consideration. First off, it's not yet another V8 build — this one has a three-rotor engine, about which the seller says absolutely nothing else. That's already pretty unique within the drag racing world, but then you come to the body of this car. It's not a dedicated top fuel look, it's not a  better-looking RX-7, it's for some reason an RX-8. I don't get it, but I do love it.

The RX-8 is probably the worst looking of Mazda's RX line (though it's still pretty good relative to any car not built by Mazda), and the structure beneath the body appears to be a totally custom tube chassis. There's no real reason to make the body look like this other than someone's enthusiasm for the model. It does get bonus points for having Han's paint job, though.

At some point in this Barracuda's life, someone wanted to take it racing. That time was long enough ago that the car no longer turns over, and judging from the construction of the half-rollcage and the design of the steering wheel we're looking at a time even before that last tank of now-skunked fuel was put in the car. Since then, some rust has bubbled up, and the tires have dry rotted away.

The car, though, still deserves that chance at being truly run ragged on track. If you can fix it up to a usable state, grind back the rust and clear out the fuel system, you'll have a classic muscle car with updated engine electronics and at least some rudimentary chassis bracing — I'm not sure how much that half cage helps the body's stiffness, but it probably doesn't hurt in the corners. Can you imagine rolling up to your local HPDE in this?

The seller lists this as an R6 quad in the ad title, but the description is more accurate: The quad is actually built around a Yamaha FZ6, the more upright cousin to the R6. I see why the seller went for the better-known bike in the listing headline, but the FZ6 is the better bike to start with for something like this. Sure, you're going to mess with the ergonomics anyway, but I'd rather start with the more comfortable bike and build out from there.

Is this quad good? I haven't ridden it to know for sure, but I'm willing to bet that it's not. The R6 engine shared by the FZ6 isn't exactly known for its low-end torque, and an off-road setup like this — an ATV on all-terrain tires — probably isn't going to see much time screaming at redline on the highway. Whether the quad is fun, though, is another question entirely. I love it when something's a little bit dumb.

Today on "Amber begs sellers to take even one decent photo of the items they're selling," I had to combine two separate shots of this Chevy Spark to get one halfway usable photo for this section. It was worth it, though, because Jalop Slack was actually fondly reminiscing about the Spark just the other day. It, too, is a car that's genuinely bad yet still kind of endearing.

I still remember the first time I drove a Spark, long before I worked here. It was bright green with a manual transmission, and upon getting in I wiggled the shifter to ensure it was in neutral, started the car, and took my foot off the clutch. It stalled. It had been in first the whole time, but the shifter had as much play in gear as my Subaru at the time had in neutral. Deeply bad car. I'm genuinely kind of considering buying this one.

Here's a truly unusual one. Not the Lada, we've had a few of those grace this hallowed column, but its history: The seller claims this is a one-owner Lada. Now, take a walk with me, because that's going to mean this car has a truly interesting story. The plate suggests this is registered, which means it's been properly imported and had all its paperwork done. Of course, given the 25-year law, it couldn't have come into the country before 2011. If this truly is a one-owner car, that means someone owned it in Russia or a former Soviet state before emigrating to the United States.

This car would have, under its current ownership, seen the fall of the Berlin wall. Its owner would have driven it through the dissolution of the Soviet Union, and into the modern establishment of whichever Iron Curtain nation it was originally sold in. Then, it and its owner would have both moved here, and watched our modern political environment. Maybe this Lada is cursed, actually.

If you're looking to get into motorcycling, now's the time. This seller is asking $2,500 for this Rebel 300 — one of the all-time great first bikes — and they've been asking that for seven weeks now. They don't want to store this all winter, a bike they're already looking to get rid of so they can upgrade. They want the thing gone, and I'm willing to bet that "No low ball" line in the ad gets less and less strict with each passing day. When the first snow falls, will this seller still want to have this bike taking up space? No.

You could likely get this bike for a song right now, and you'd end up with one of the best beginner bikes ever made. There's a reason these are beloved by MSF courses — this one could well be a former MSF bike, given its aftermarket replacements for the mirrors that instructors would've removed. If you have space to throw a bike through the winter (or warm enough gear to ride through the cold), you can get a screaming deal on a first motorcycle right here.

Back in the day, I had a friend with a Fiat 500 Abarth much like this one. Theirs was wrapped purple and tuned, with aftermarket intake piping powdercoated in Tiffany Blue, but beyond that it was just like this. That little Fiat was one of the most fun cars I ever got to ride in, and I think you should try one out for yourself.

Stock 500 Abarths are fun, but a tune really wakes them up. This wide-eyed little gummy candy of a car suddenly becomes a small rocket bound for low earth orbit, and you're left to do nothing but hang on and laugh manically until you get back to the Starbucks parking lot that hosts your car meet. Then, with the engine up to temperature, you can stuff the back half of your Chipotle burrito into the intake piping to reheat it. What other car has a built-in microwave for your leftovers?

Back in the day, there was a rule on Dopest: If I saw a kei car, it went in the column. That's gone by the wayside as more and more kei cars have showed up on Marketplace, with ground ceded to my rules against double-dipping on automakers, but this Sambar deserved the honor once so freely doled out to its brethren — if anything, an honor made all the more meaningful by its newfound rarity.

These kei cars technically aren't legal to register in New York State, but some folks seem to get them through the system. This one avoids the rules by being located in Jersey, but it deserves to be able to come to the greatest city in the world. I deserve to be able to drive one of these to the crag, is what I'm saying. If I can't, though, you should do so in my stead.

Motorola’s $1,599 Signature 27 Is the ‘World’s Fastest Android Smartphone’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Motorola’s $1,599 Signature 27 Is the ‘World’s Fastest Android Smartphone’

After numerous teases and even a sponsored YouTube hands-on video, Motorola has finally, thankfully, fully unveiled its new flagship smartphone, the Signature 27. It is being billed as the world’s fastest Android smartphone. It’s certainly among the most expensive slab phones.

The Motorola Signature 27 runs on the Snapdragon 8 Elite Extreme Gen 6, Qualcomm’s leading mobile chip. It has an eight-core CPU, six performance cores, and a suite of advanced features. It’s seriously powerful.

And while that’s great, and performance is definitely a significant focus for the Motorola Signature 27, that’s only part of the story. Motorola is pulling out all the stops.

“The world’s best camera system launched in 2026, outranking all other Android and iOS devices, as assessed by DxOMark.”

“The brightest display available in a smartphone.”

“The only smartphone in-market with Audio by Bang & Olufsen.”

Looking first at the camera system claim, DxOMark did, in fact, rate the Motorola Signature 27 as the second-best smartphone camera system on the market, behind only the Huawei Pura 80 Ultra, which launched last year. The Signature 27 is just ahead of the Apple iPhone 18 Pro.

As PetaPixel has written a couple of times now, the Motorola Signature 27 has a 50-megapixel Sony Lytia 910 main camera with LOFIC technology, promising expanded dynamic range and better low-light image quality, all else equal. The Lytia 910 is a relatively large Type 1/1.28 image sensor that has proven very good in the few phones that feature it.

The Signature 27 also has a 200-megapixel periscope telephoto camera with 100x Super Zoom and a 50-megapixel ultrawide camera. Motorola doesn’t say who makes these image sensors, but it does say the telephoto is a Type 1/1.56 and the ultrawide has a Type 1/2.76 sensor. These are fairly standard sizes among high-end phones.

But wait, it sure looks like there’s a fourth camera on the back of the Signature 27. Good eye, hypothetical person. This is the first ever dedicated “EVS action sensor” on a smartphone. EVS stands for “Event Vision Sensor,” and it detects pixel-level changes in light intensity in real time and works alongside Action Mode to freeze fast-action subjects more effectively. It’s like an assistant rather than a dedicated camera. It will be very interesting to see how well it works in the real world. PetaPixel plans to get its hands on the Signature 27 for a comprehensive review.

Then there’s the display, the world’s brightest. It’s a new 6.9-inch LTPO Extreme AMOLED that features Tandem OLED technology. It combines two emissive layers into one and promises up to 12,000 nits of peak brightness. Unsurprisingly, there’s a caveat. There always is. This is localized peak brightness, not full-screen brightness, and will almost assuredly be limited to very specific situations. Nonetheless, that’s crazy bright.

As for the claim that the Motorola Signature 27 is the only in-market phone with Audio by Bang & Olufsen, that’s pretty straightforward. Motorola recently signed a multi-year partnership with the audio brand, and the Signature 27 features B&O’s audio expertise in both hardware and software. Motorola touts an all-new speaker architecture that sounds truly fantastic.

To no one’s surprise, the premium, flagship Motorola Signature 27 will cost a pretty penny when it arrives on November 19. The Signature 27 is $1,599, which puts it near the very top of the slab phone price bracket, and certainly places it among the most expensive smartphones available in the U.S. Speaking of which, it’s great to see a high-end Android smartphone that isn’t made by Samsung or Google get an American release. They are few and far between.

Writing code got faster. The work around it didn’t. See how you can plan, write code, review changes, and investigate production issues with GitHub Copilot, and what work still needs your judgment.

This October, GitHub saw more than 1.2 billion commits in one week—over 400% more than a year earlier. This is more commits than GitHub received in all of 2025. Chances are, you’re generating more code than you were a year ago. But ask yourself: do you feel 400% more productive than you were last year?

Building software takes more than a good prompt. As AI speeds up implementation, software developers are feeling more pressure in other places: a Faros AI study found that pull requests spent 441% longer in review when teams went from low to high AI adoption.

As a software developer, you gather requirements and steer implementation, review changes and evaluate test results, then investigate what happens in production. Agents can help you work more efficiently across all of these stages: Plan, Build, Verify, and Observe. We’ll walk through what an effective agentic engineering workflow looks like on GitHub.

See if this sounds familiar. You’re planning an API change. The design is in one tool, a compatibility requirement is buried in Slack, and an old pull request explains why the current implementation works this way. Before you can write a useful plan, you need to connect those details, compare approaches, and agree on how existing clients will keep working. What looked like a straightforward planning task has become hours of searching, cross-checking, and chasing answers. You’re exhausted before implementation even begins.

You can use Copilot to pull that scattered context into one place, work through the tradeoffs, and turn the decisions into an implementation plan.

What took days of searching and context gathering now produces a reviewable plan, explicit acceptance criteria, and scoped issues, with your time spent on the decisions instead of the retrieval. Then, once you settle on an approach, you’ve already set yourself and your agents up to implement your plan.

You’re ready to turn that plan into code. But life has other ideas. Your agent is halfway through a task, your next meeting starts in five minutes, and your dog has been staring at the door for twenty. Then someone in the meeting points out an edge case the implementation needs to handle. Everyone needs something from you at once. You’d like to keep making progress, but you can’t be in three places at the same time.

You can keep your work moving without being tied to the computer where you started it.

Writing code is more than shooting off a single prompt to your LLM and seeing what you get back. And with more work on your plate than ever, it can be difficult to maintain your coding standards and deliver your projects on time. Copilot helps tasks progress independently with shared project knowledge, while letting you pick up a session where you left off across devices. That gives you more room to work through implementation decisions and handle changes as they come up. And as your work finishes, you have focused changes ready to review.

After spending an hour wrangling your agents to get them moving in the right direction, you think you’ve all landed in a good spot. So you open a pull request labeled “small refactor.” It changes 47 files. You wanted feedback on shared validation logic, but it’s buried among updates to every endpoint that calls it, formatting changes, and several hundred lines of generated tests. You wonder how long it will take to get an approval on this, given the endless list of pull requests on your team’s review board.

That review queue is the 441% in practice. You can shorten the wait from both ends: make your changes easier for a person to review, and let Copilot clear the routine checks and fixes before anyone opens the diff.

Smaller changes with the routine work already handled means fewer rounds of back-and-forth. Your reviewers get to spend their attention on whether the code does what you intended, and what could go wrong.

Your change gets approved, the deployment checks pass, and you finally get to cross it off your list. The next morning, your on-call dashboard shows intermittent timeouts, and support reports that a few large customers are affected. Now you’re comparing logs, release history, and customer reports while someone asks whether you should roll back. “All checks passed” feels less reassuring than it did yesterday.

Bring operational evidence and engineering context together so you can decide what to do.

Figuring out what happened after a release can leave you bouncing between dashboards, conversations, and commit history. With Copilot helping connect that information, you can document what you found, create follow-up issues, and improve the guidance available for future tasks. You spend more time deciding what needs to change and less time retracing the investigation. And your next plan starts with what you learned from the software people are actually using.

Take one piece of work already on your plate and look beyond the coding task. What context does it need? How will you review and test it? How will you know it worked after it ships?

You don’t have to change your whole process at once. Start with something that frustrates you most this week and put an agent on it. Then expand to other parts of your process over time. Once agents are helping across your whole workflow, you might finally feel as productive as your commit count says you are.

We covered this topic live at GitHub Universe 2026 in “Agentic Engineering: Past the Pilot.”

Page Stunner Helps Leicester City Avoid FA Cup Upset Against Oxford City

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A clinical second 45 minutes cancelled out two first-half wonder goals, and ultimately helped 2021’s FA Cup winners Leicester City to avoid a shock exit from this year's competition at the first hurdle.

Louis Page’s stunner broke the deadlock; however, Oxford City’s Scott Pollock came up with his own moment of magic to level up proceedings, scoring direct from a corner.

In the second half, the goals kept coming, though Leicester’s quality proved too much for a defiant Oxford side.

The game got off to a fairly tepid start, with Page’s stunning effort after 16 minutes proving to be the catalyst for the game springing into life.

The teenager found the ball way outside the area, but opted to take aim, and rightly so, as his effort nestled into the top left corner.

Oxford soon responded with chances of their own. Ellis Brown was a major threat on the right-hand side, winning plenty of corners. In the 31st minute, one of them proved crucial. Boyhood Leicester fan Scott Pollock whipped the ball into the area and the delicious cross evaded the clusters of men in the area and ultimately sailed into the far corner. Franco Ravizolli was in no man’s land; Pollock was in dreamland.

Between the equaliser and half-time, Leicester looked to take the sting out of the game. They slowed down proceedings, whilst also attempting to quieten down the partisan atmosphere.

After the break, Russell Martin opted for an offensive change with winger Harry Howell replacing Caleb Okoli. It was a move that was soon vindicated. By the 53rd minute, the Brighton loanee got to the byline, before delivering a ball which was wonderfully met by Emil Riis, who headed it home.

Six minutes later, Leicester had a third. Kamari Doyle’s outside-the-foot finish beat Jonny Maxted, giving the Foxes a much-needed two-goal cushion.

However, just after the hour mark, Oxford showed they weren’t going down without a fight. Captain Josh Ashby was found in behind, and from there he found the back of the net.

Ultimately, the comeback was short-lived, though. With 10 minutes of normal time to go, Tommy Watson found a fourth. The winger's footwork proved too tricky, and despite the angle, he managed to poke the ball into the far corner.

That ensured Leicester made it through to the second round of the cup, but it didn’t come easy. The likes of Howell and Watson changed the game, helping overcome a tough Oxford side.

The FA Cup can offer life-changing prize funds for teams lower down the football pyramid.

Nearly 100 Avalanches Have Already Been Recorded in Colorado. Here’s What Early-Season Skiers Should Know.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Early-season snow blankets the San Juan Mountains above 11,000 feet in southwestern Colorado, where unusually warm, wet storms have contributed to avalanche activity this fall. (Photo: Colorado Avalanche Information Center)

An avalanche swept a skier an estimated 500 feet down a mountainside near Silverton, Colorado, on November 3, partially burying him before his partner dug him out. The skier, whose name and age are unknown, was unharmed, according to professional skiier and filmmaker Colter Hinchliffe, who captured the entire ordeal on video.

The incident is among several early-season avalanche close calls in the Mountain West so far in 2026. Each serves as a reminder that even when snow cover is thin, slide danger can still be massive.

On October 25, a skier triggered a wind-slab avalanche on Colorado’s Mount Bross that buried them facedown before companions dug them out. On November 1, skiers near Alta, Utah, triggered multiple wet-snow slides that ran over a cliff and reached a service road, according to an observation submitted to the Utah Avalanche Center.

As of November 6, the Colorado Avalanche Information Center (CAIC) recorded nearly 100 avalanches statewide, including 15 triggered by skiers, Brian Lazar, CAIC deputy director, told Outside.

“That amount of avalanches at this time of year is not that unusual,” Lazar said. “We’re in avalanche season, even though the snowpack is still developing, and it’s important to acknowledge that, even when there’s a lot of dry ground showing.”

Hinchliffe said heavy early snowfall has made the San Juan Mountains an especially popular destination for early-season skiers.

“A lot of people in the mountains means a lot of decisions are being made, and some of them can go wrong,” Hinchliffe told Outside. “That’s kind of what we saw.”

The weather has also contributed to unstable snow conditions across Colorado, Lazar said. Two recent warm, particularly wet storms brought rain to elevations above 12,000 feet in parts of southwestern Colorado, triggering wet avalanches. Between storms, dry periods allowed weak layers of snow to develop on the snow surface, which fresh snowfall later buried.

Lazar warned that early-season avalanches can be dangerous even when they’re relatively small, particularly with so little snow covering the terrain.

“Even if you get knocked off your feet and take a short ride in a small avalanche, it might not be big enough to bury or kill you, but it can drag you across rocks, logs, stumps, and things like that,” Lazar said. “Early-season conditions can produce some really nasty rides, even in small avalanches.”

Shallow snow can also lead skiers into more dangerous terrain.

“If you’re going out looking to ride on snow, you are naturally drawn to the places with the best coverage, and those are the same places that often have the most dangerous avalanche conditions,” Lazar said.

Before heading out, Lazar recommends checking the local avalanche forecast, carrying a transceiver, shovel, and probe, and traveling with partners who know how to use them. He also emphasized the importance of avalanche education and regular rescue practice.

Hinchliffe said he and his touring partner had deliberately agreed to take a conservative approach that morning, sticking to mellower terrain rather than testing steeper slopes.

“At this point, I’ve got the whole season laid out in front of me,” he said. ” So when I ski in the early season, I try to be very cautious with my choices.”

Re-releases of Wing Commander, Ultima titles hit Steam and GOG

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s mostly bad news from EA lately, but preservation efforts are nice to see.

Many people are not big fans of game publisher EA right now for good reason, but for those who remember a better time in the 1990s, there’s a little good news. Twenty-five classic games from that EA era have been lightly remastered and updated, then placed for sale on Steam and GOG, including several Wing Commander games and nearly the entire Ultima series.

Below is the full list of remastered or re-released games:

In a lengthy blog post published this week, Jim Vessella, a senior producer at EA, described the efforts that went into remastering Wing Commander I-IV and Prophecy, Privateer 2, and Road Rash.

It’s a long, detailed story that’s worth reading yourself, but here’s a top-level summary. After working on the mostly widely acclaimed Command & Conquer: Remastered Collection in 2019, Vessella writes that he learned about and visited a room at EA’s Redwood Shores campus that contained a large archive of poorly labeled or sorted physical media from ages past.

In his free time, and with the help of others, he began cataloging this archive and discovered, among other things, that it contained virtually the entirety of the original masters of Wing Commander III and Wing Commander IV‘s full-motion-video cutscenes.

The story that ensues involves building a small team of contractors, poring through the footage 17 hours a day on swing shifts to label and digitize it, and then coordinating with several key community members—particularly Matt Wells, who has made community enhancement patches for some of the Wing Commander games—to put together remasters of those games.

The flagship offering of those remasters is much, much higher-quality full-motion video sequences than have ever been available before, but other enhancements include 60 frames-per-second gameplay, a plethora of newly available .INI tweaks, and full controller support (though looking at the Steam forums for the games, some folks are having trouble with said controller support).

For Privateer 2—the little-remembered sequel to Wing Commander: Privateer—the crew couldn’t find the original, high-quality remasters of the FMV scenes, and ended up getting in touch with the game’s original cinematics director, Steve Hilliker, who had the tapes in his attic. Hilliker had always wanted to take a new color-grading pass, and he did just that, while also remastering and upscaling all of the cutscenes.

Similar updates were given to Wing Commander Prophecy. The blog post also details GOG’s involvement in restoring and remastering Prophecy and Road Rash. For the latter, EA re-licensed the entire original soundtrack for re-release.

The e collection is on sale on GOG and Steam right now; previous owners of the old versions of the games on GOG and the EA storefront get these remasters for free. None of the games cost more than $5 each.

One good story doesn’t undo a lot of bad news and questionable decisions from EA, but this shows that a few passionate people can do something remarkable for game preservation, even if some of the work was done on a volunteer basis outside normal working hours. (EA committed some resources to the restorations as the project progressed, though.)

While the remasters aren’t perfect, the gaming industry could use news like this. It’s worth reading the full story in the blog post for a journey down memory lane and a look at how involved a restoration process like this can be.

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

A permanent Arc Raiders PvE mode would be easy to do, says Embark CEO, “maybe people can choose"

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Arc Raiders developer Embark Studios has yet to come out and say that a permanent PvE mode is coming to the game, but we're certainly hearing more and more rumblings in that direction.

In a new interview with The Game Business, Embark CEO turned Nexon games chief Patrick Söderlund joined Arc Raiders executive producer Aleksander Grøndal to discuss, among other things, the reception to the game's recent PvE mode test.

For a limited time, players could opt into PvE-only rounds of Arc Raiders to enjoy the game's world without the risk of another raider gunning them down. Reception seemed mixed but overall positive, and it seems Embark is warming up to the idea of PvE as a whole. Importantly, the studio has also reaffirmed that it wouldn't be a hard thing to add.

"We could just frankly add PvE," says Söderlund. "It's not a difficult thing for us to do, but we didn't want to obstruct or fiddle too much with the core loop of the game. The PvP aspect of a PvP PvE game does add, we believe, adequate tension."

Maintaining the intrigue and threat baked into Arc Raiders is part of the hesitance to commit to PvE, especially given Arc Raiders' origins as a PvE-only game that pivoted to extraction PvP following troubled production.

While Embark previously felt this type of radical change to the "emotional loop" of Arc Raiders is "maybe not the right thing to do," Söderlund continues, "we've now since tried it and realized that it's fun. Why not have it? You know, maybe people can choose what they like."

"People may have been wondering, 'Why the hell aren't they just doing what some players were looking for?' We were looking at the holistic picture, which we have to do, and the whole of the game to also protect the creative integrity of what we have created, what we aspire to build, and what the game should be according to us."

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

Here, too, live service development complicates things. The Arc Raiders community is so big, Söderlund reasons, that "we can't ignore the PvE camp" completely. The goal now is to "do a good enough job to cater to all the player types without watering down the overall experience."

Grøndal agrees that PvE could potentially fit into the game. He cites the popularity of the PvE test: almost 50% of games played during the test were PvE. That is a huge number, and while some people did "change their mind" about PvE being fun, you do "have players who absolutely loved it."

Again, he comes back to PvE not hurting "the integrity of the experience itself." This is where, beyond the apparently simple technical implementation, the broader implications of PvE are likely more complicated – economy, population, servers, event design, and so on.

Arc Raiders devs know PvE has been "polarizing," promise to prioritize the game's "overall health and enjoyability" when deciding what to do next
